Appeal  from  Summary  Decisions  of 


We  are  glad  to  perceive  that  some  attempt 
it  now  making  to  remedy  the  great  want  ex- 
perienced io  the  colony,  of  a  proper  system  of 
appeals  against  the  almost  irresponsible  deci- 
sions of  magistrates  engaged  iu  dispensing  jos- 
tieo  amongst  us.   A  tendency  has  showo  itself 
of  late  years  in  England,  and  one  which 
tbiok  eboold    be  most  zealously  guarded 
against— to  leave  a  great  deal  of  criminal  and 
quasi-criminal  justice  to  be  dealt  with  by  a 
magistrate  in  a  summary  manner.   We  can- 
not help  regarding  every  movement  of  this 
kind  as  a  departure  from  the  venerable  Eng- 
lish principle  that  every  man  should  be  tried 
by  a  jury  of  bis  peers.  There  is  too  much  of 
a  tendency  to  imperialism— to  autocracy— in 
it.   But  since  a  great  deal  cf  oar  law  is  to 
be  administered  iu  that  way.  it  should  be  our 
dtHyto.ee  that  the  evils  to  be  appended 
from  an  abuse  ol  magisterial  power  should  be 
obviated  as  much  as  possible  by  giving  the 
accused  a  further  hearing  and  before  another 
Court.   We  all  know  how  long  the  colony 
has  been  without  a  sufficient  number  of  magis- 
trate* to  constitute  a  Court  of  Quarter  Ses- 
sions.  Up  to  the  time  of  the  election  of  the 
Mayor,  and  for  a  long  time  previous,  the 
Magisterial  power  in  this  community  could 
be  exercised  only  by  our  Police  Commissioner, 
from  whose  decision,  as  a  general  thing, 
there  was  not  a  single  court  to  appeal  to. 
Since  the  election    of   the  Mayor,  what- 
ever may  be  the  cause,  we  fure  no  better. 
The  necessity  then  of  some  such  rights  as 
those  given  by  Mr.  Ring  in  his  Bill  lately 
introduced  into  the  House  of  Assembly  is 
quite  apparent.    It  is  proposed  to  give  a  right 
to  appeal  from  the  derision  of  a  stipendiary 
or  other  magistrate  in  all  oases  summarily 
adjadicsted  upon.    The  appeal  is  to  be  to 
the  Supreme  Court,  bat  is  not  to  do  away 
with  the  ordinary  right  of  appeal  to  the  Court 
of  Quarter  Sessions  when  such  a  court  ex- 
ists. '  The  appealed  cause  may  be  heard 
eight  days  after  notice  of  appeal  shall  bsve 
been  given  to  the  Supreme  Court  The 
notice  itself  must  contain  a  synopsis  of  the 
grounds  ot  appeal.    The  costs,  however, 
mast  be  guaranteed  before  anytbiog  can  be 
done.   Th>s  will  have  the  tendency  to  pre- 
vent frivolous  and  gronndlesa  applications 
for  reversal  of  the  decision  of  the  inferior 
magistrate.   The  bill  also  provides  that  an 
accused,  whenever  charged  before  a  magis- 
trate with  a  cause,  may  demand  to  be  tried 
by  a  jury  and  not  by  the  magistrate,  and 
ehall  have  the  right  to  have  his  case  sent  to 
the  Quarter  Sessions  or  Supreme  Court  for 
trial,  and  in  the  meantime  to  be  admitted  to 
bail  in  bailable  cases.   On  the  whole  w-s  are 
inclined  to  believe  the  change  sought  to  be 
effected  in  the  present  unsatisfactory  and 
irresponsible  system  of  administeriogjustioe 
•will  be  found  to  be  of  the  utmost  benefit  to 
the  inhabitants  of  the  Colony,  as  it  will  go 
far  UmJo  away  with  any  evil  effect  of  wrong 
magisterial  decisions,  whet  her  they  be  caused 
inadvertently,  or  by  wilfulness,  ignorance, 
or  incompetence.

Washisuton,  Oct.  18. — A  dispatch  from 
head  quarters  of  the  army  of  the  Potomac 
says  tne  recounoisfances  under  Hancock 
and  Humphrey,  returned  after  going  within 
five  miles  or  Bunker  Hill.  The  enemy  did  not 
interlere  with  the  return  of  Hancock,  but  fol- 
lowed Humphrey  with  artillery  and  cavalry 
to  within  a  short  distance  of  Shepardstowu, 
opening  on  him  with  artillery  wherever  tbey 
could.  Our  loss  wus  one  killed  and  six  wound- 
ed. The  troops  crossed  the  Potomac  safely 
last  night  at  Shepardstown  with  a  number  of 
prisoners.  One  hundred  rehel  wounded  left 
at  Cbarlestown  were  brought  within  ottr  lines 
uud  will  be  paroled.  The  rebels  in  disputing 
Humphrey  bad  30  or  40  killed. 

Lot.-iBVii.LK.  Oct.  18.— A  gentleman  who  left 
Charleston  on  the  20th  Sept.,  gives  a  gloomy 
picture  of  affairs  there.  All  the  necessaries  of 
ife  are  at  starvation  prices.  Large  amounts 
of  counterfeit  Confederate  notes  are  in  circula- 
tion. He  says  the  Charleslooians  have  com- 
peted two  formidable  rams  which  will  be  put 
into  service  as  soon  us  men  can  be  obtained.

Emtob  British  Colonist  :— As  we  have  in 
our  KnglUh  papers  "  Sketches  of  Parliament."  I 
do  not  seo  why  yon  colonists  should  not  have 
yours  also.    I  thought  I  would  take  a  peep  at  the 

firocecding*  of  the  "collective  wisdom"  of  the 
sland  and  give  you  my  English  ideas  of  them. 
I  wa*  at  a  loss  to  conceive  how  doctors  should 
have  any  greater  claim  to  exemption  from  payiuK 
licences  than  lawers  unles*  it  is  that  Ave  make  I 
quorum  in  your  Assembly,  and  there  are  three 
doctors  in  it.    These  gentlemen  certainly  deserve 
to  have  L.  L  D.  attached  to  their  name  as  well  as 
M.  D.,  for  they  seem  to  perfectly  understand  the 
first  law  of  taking  care  of  themselves.   The  leKal 
gentlemen  do  not  appear  to  be  any  way  deficient 
iu  that  particular,  for  one  of  them  with  the  view 
no  doubt,  of  increaaing  litigation,  proposes  the 
right  of  sppeal  in  summary  convictions,  alleging 
as  his  reason  for  doing  *o,  a  provision  aaainst 
circumstances  that  never  have  occurred  here,  nor 
are  at  all  likely  to  do  so.    Bat  the  most  extraor- 
dinary feature  of  the  proceeding*  of  the  22d  in*t,

not  contemplate  any  such  charge.   Thu*.  the  in 
feronce  would  be  that  the  Chancellor  of  the  Ex- 
chequer had  been  receiving  into  the  treasury  two

larly.

yeara.

become  an  Imperial  question, 
so.  We  believe  that  there  are  two  great 
principles  that  ought  to  lay  at  the  foundation 
of  the  policy  of  this  country,  and  they  are  free 
trade  for  this  province  and  union  with  British 
Columbia. 

If  snch  be  not  the  case,  a  grave  error  will  be 
committed,  before  which  all  our  grievancea 
will  be  aa  nothing.  If  a  delegate  ia  to  be  sent 
to  England  to  get  rid  of  the  incubus  that  has 
paralysed  our  progress,  send  a  man  who  is 
sound  on  both  prinoiplea.  Such  •  man  is 
Waddington  for  instance ;  certainly  not  a 
person  who  has  declared  not  only  against  a 
union  of  the  provinces  bat  against  onr  free- 
port  system.  Beaides,  if  we  send  a  dele- 
gate to  England,  let  as  send  a  man  of  some 
substance,  not  a  gentleman  without  a  atake 
in  the  country.  When  Mr.  MoClure's  hat 
is  on,  his  bouse  ia  tbatehed.  Like  the  dele- 
gale  from  British  Colombia,  be  will  have 
no  interest  in  the  country  beyond  the  grati- 
fication of  a  boyish  ambition.  He  will  be  at

MINING  NEWS. 

LIOBTMIMO  t'RKEK. 

A  reliable  gentleman  receotly  from  Light- 
ning Creek,  which  he  left  on  the  7th  Oct., 
informs  us  that  only  four  companies  were 
at  work  when  he  left ;  the  German  Co.,  Cali- 
fornia Co.,  Campbell  Co.,  and  the  Shaft  Co. 
The  latter  is  supposed  to  be  the  best  claim 
on  the  creek.  The  shaft  from  which  it 
takes  its  name  is  sunk  35  feet  in  the  side  of 
the  hill.  With  three  men  drifting  they  have 
taken  out  130  ox.  in  a  day ;  bat  about  100  oz. 
is  a  fair  average  of  their  work.  The  Cali- 
fornia Co.  baa  struck  the  same  lead  by  run- 
ning a  tunnel  into  the  hill  from  their  creek 
claim— the  bed  of  tbe  ereek.  They  took  out 
a  lump  weighing  14%  ox.  The  German  Co. 
baa  been  working  ever  since  spring  in  tbia 
ereek.  It  is  supposed  they  averaged  40  oz. 
per  day.  The  Campbell  Co.  have  worked 
out  the  poorest  of  their  ground  ;  but  next  year 
ihey  expect  it  to  pay  as  well  aa  any  claim 
in  Cariboo.  They  know  it  is  riob  beyond  a 
doubt.  It  averaged  about  70  oz.  a  day  from 
the  5th  July  to  the  time  our  informant  left. 
Over  9100,000  has  been  taken  out  of  this 
claim  in  three  months.  The  expense  of 
opening  it  this  year  has  been  S25, 
000,  so  there  will  be  no  expense  for  opening 
next  spring. 

Good  bench  diggings  were  discovered 
about  the  middle  of  September  on  the  hill's 
side  above  the  Campbell  Company.  It  is 
on  the  opposite  side  from  the  Shaft  Com- 
pany, but  farther  down.  They  prospect  well 
rom  50  cents  to  $5  in  the  gravel.  It  is 
supposed  to  be  the  old  bed  of  a  ereek.  The 
bench  is  about  200  feet  above  the  level  of 
tbe  bed  of  the  ereek.  That  shows  there  is 
no  danger  of  the  country  wearing  out. 

Any  one  may  lake  a  pao  and  find  the 
color  of  gold  anywhere  in  the  bill  aide  of 
Lightning  Creek. 

The  Upper  Cafion  claim  paid  tolerably 
well.  There  is  not  a  claim  between  the 
Campbell  claim  and  the  upper  canon,  but 
must  be  a  success  —  tbe  distance  be- 
tween those  points  is  about  two  miles. 

The  benoh  diggings  and  tunnels  have 
opened  a  new  era  on  Lightning  Creek.  The 
miner  will  not  bo  troubled  with  water  aa 
they  are  in  the  creek. 

Tha  Campbell  Company  took  oat  $4000 
from  thee  feet  square  on  the  surface  of  the 
bed  rock  111  . 

Fifteen  or  twenty  persons  are  expected  to 
winter  on  the  creek. 

There  are  several  parties  who  intend  to 
prospect  in  the  hills  during  the  winter.

COLONIAL  NEWS. 


CANADA.  # 

The  Hon.  C.  C.  S.  DeBleury  died  lately  in 
Montreal.  He  was  well  known  as  one  of  the 
oldest  members  of  tbe  Lower  Canadian  Bar, 
having  been  admitted  in  1819. 

An  American  company  are  negotiating  with 
Mr.  Davies,  tbe  proprietor  of  the  Acton  copper 
mines,  for  tbe  purchase  of  that  property.  Mr. 
Davies  asks  $300,000.  and  tbe  company  have 
offered  a  sum  not  far  from  that  amount. 

In  consequence  of  the  additional  excise 
duty  of  $1  per  barrel  on  beer,  now  required 
to  be  paid  to  the  Government,  Canadian  brew- 
ers have  resolved  to  raise  the  price  on  that 
beverage. 

Mr.  T.  H.  McLean,  of  Enniskillen,  C.  W  . 
has  just  struck  what  he  calls  tbe  Leviathan 
oil  well  near  bis  refinery  in  that  place.  Tbe 
estimate  yield  is  about  4000  bbls  per  day. 

William  Bartlett,  one  of  tbe  councillors  of 
East  Whitby,  lately  committed  suicide  by 
hanging  himself*  in  his  own  barn.  Mental 
despondency  was  alleged  to  be  the  cause. 

A  farmer  named  Louis  Bousquin  died  re- 
cently in  the  township  of  Kin^sey  (Artba- 
baska)  at  the  advanced  age  of  106  years.  Not- 
withstanding  his  great  age,  he  retained  bis 
mental  faculties  to  tbe  last. 

The  Hon.  L.  H.  Holton  has  been  elected  by 
acclamation  (or  the  Victoria  Division  of  tin 
Legislative  Council.  Mr.  McCrae  ia  tbe  suc- 
cessful candidate  of  tbe  Western  Division  by 
quite  a  majority  over  bis  opponent,  Mr.  Prince. 

The  Bank  of  Montreal  has  decided  to  estab- 
lish agencies  in  the  following  towns :  St. 
Mary's,  Elora,  Fergus,  and  Mount  Forrest — a 
judicious  selection  of  places  in  which  to  ex- 
tend business. 

A  Mr.  Grier,  in  tbe  employment  of  Swin- 
burne &  Co.,  of  Montreal,  was  arrested  on  a 
charge  of  embezzlement  in  Cornwall,  whither 
he  had  absconded,  but  upon  examination  was 
discharged. 

Tbe  Commissioners  on  tbe  Ottawa  building 
have  thus  far  examined  nine  witnesses,  whose 
evidence  covers  three  hundred  foolscap  pages. 
It  is  thought  tbe  labors  of  tbe  Commission 
will  soon  terminate. 

Col.  Lysons  has  been  appointed  Depaty 
Qoarter-Master  General,  in  lieu  of  Colonel 
McKenzie,  and  will  shortly  take  np  his  quar- 
ters in  Montreal. 

Quite  an  excitement  has  been  created  lately 
in  Dundas,  on  account  of  several  bodies  hav- 
ing been  stolen  from  tbe  cemetry  for  dissection. 

Mr.  Leonard,  of  London,  C.  W.,  has  been 
elected  member  of  tbe  Legislative  Council  for 
the  Malahide  Division  by  a  majority  of  218 
over  Mr.  Lecher,  tbe  Conservative  candidate.

Another  military  murder  has  been  com- 
mitted at  Quebec,  making  the  third  which  has 
been  perpetrated  within  a  short  time.  A  por- 
tiou  of  the  l?th  regiment  were  on  parade,

his  rifle  and  fired  at  sergeant  William  Ryall, 
killing  bim  instantly.  No  cause  whatever  is 
assigned  for  the  fatal  deed.

Tuesday,  October  «§7  18419. 
News  from  the  Stataa. 

Wo  la;  before  our  readers  eleven  days 
later  news  from  the  Slates.  We  do  nut  see  the 
slightest  indication  of  peace.    The  Federals 
have  been  successful  in  a  battle  near  Perrys- 
ville,  Kentucky,  and  the  suocess  of  Rose- 
krans  at  Corinth  seems  to  be  fully  confirmed. 
In  the  Western  department  matters  favor 
the  north  ;  and  to  ujake  it  more  conclusively 
in  their  favor,  Halleck  is  to  be  sent  out  West 
as  Commander-in-Chief.    The  last  winter 
campaign  may  possibly  be  re-enacted.  In 
the  East  the  tide  of  events  seems  to  be  on  the 
Confederate  side.    McClellan  is  doing  noth- 
ing.  Lee  has  200,000  on  the  Rappahannock. 
So  the  expulsion  from  Maryland  seems  to 
have  produced  uo  very  marked  results,  whilst 
the  East  is  balanced  by  the  West.   A  for- 
ward movement  of  the  Federals  was  rumored. 
Another  change  in  the  Cabinet  is  to  occur — 
Banks  is  to  take  Stanton's  place  as  Secretary 
of  War.   The  Southern  Congress  are  disposed 
to  raise  the  standard  of  civil  war  in  Oregon 
and  California.   The  North  seems  to  want  a 
head— a  dictator  ;  and  if  success  does  not  soon 
follow  the  plans  of  the  Cabinet,  it  will  not  be 
long  before  the  army  will  impose  a  dictator 
on  the  nation.   It  will  be  dictator  against 
dictator.   The  northern  people  are  dissatis- 
fied ;  and  any  change  of  rulers  promising 
success  and  peace  will  prove  acceptable.  Gold 
fumumndu  a  premium  of  33  per  cent,  in  New 
Vork,  and  Federal  treasury  notes  are  nearly 
30  per  cent  below  par.   Rather  gloomy  finan- 
cial prospects. 


English  New*. 

By  the  arrival  of  the  steamer  Sierra  Ne- 
vada we  have  details  of  English  news  to  Sep- 
tember 6th,  being  one  week  later  than  the  last. 
pWe  perceive  that  Her  Majesty,  by  an  order 
in  Council,  has  authorised  Horatio  Nelson 
Lay  and  Captain  Sberard  Osborn  to  enter  the 
service  of  the  Emperor  or  China  and  to  enlist 
British  subjects  to  fight  in  that  country.  Mr. 
Lay,  who  represents  the  Chinese  Government 
in  England,  and  Capt.  Osborn,  who  is  to  be 
Commander-in-chief  *f  the  new  auxilary  forces, 
are  making  arrangements  for  the  formation  of 
a  fleet.   Quite  a  number  of  war  vessels  have 
already  been  purchased  by  them  and  orders 
given  lor  more.   The  International  Temper- 
ance Couveniion  had  commenced  its  sittings 
at  Hauover  square  Rooms,  London,  onder  the 
presidency  of  Sir  Walter  C.  Trevelyari.  The 
plan  of  th3  convention  is  very  much  like  that 
of  the  Social  Science  Congress.   The  South 
Austral;an  Parliament  having  addressed  the 
Imperial  Government.  prayiDg  the  dismissal  of 
Judge  Boothbry  for  erroneous  decisions,  it  was 
submitted  to  the  law  officers  of  the  Crown, 
who  considered,  taking  all  circumstances  into 
consideration,  that  the  Crown  would  not  be 
well  advised  to  accede  to  the  address.   A  Liv- 
erpool ship  owner,  named  Ruxton,  has  been 
committed  for  trial,  charged  with  being  ac- 
cessory to  the  wilful  destruction  of  one  of  his 
own  vessels,  with  the  intention  of  defrauding 
the  underwriters.   The  vessel,  which  was  called 
the  Roscoe,  was  destroyed  by  fire,  according 
to  bis  orders,  when  within  a  few  miles  of 
Smyrna.   From  the  Manchester  Guardian  we 
learn  that  Miss  M.  S.  Rye,  so  well  known  in 
connection  with  the  Female  Middle  Class  Emi- 
gration Society  is  now  endeavoring  to  send 
out  to  British  Columbia  a  number  of  those 
young  women  who  have  recently  been  thrown 
out  of  employment  in  Manchester.   She  has 
now  upon  her  books  over  a  thousand  names  of 
young  women,  principally  of  the  working 
classes,  whose  ages  rauge  from  18  to  30.  The 
ordinary  passage-money  to  British  Columbia 
is  £25.   Mr.  Lindsay,  M.  P.  has  consented  to 
reduce  the  fare  by  £&  each  of  Miss  Rye's 
nominees,  and  has  also  arranged  that  if  Miss 
Rye  will  pay  £10  for  each  yourlg  woman  she 
sends  be  will  take  an  L  O.  U.  from  the  girl 
herself  for  the  remaiuder.    Miss  Rye  had,  in 
consequence,  determined  if  possible,  to  send 
fifty  young    women  from    Manchester  to 
British  Columbia  on  the  15tb  September* 
AnJAgriculturist  who  has  travelled  through" 
the  country,  writing  to  the  limes,  says,  in 
•peaking  of  the  crops,  «  my  opinion  as  to  the 
general  produce  of  ibis  harvest  in  England 
is  that  we  shall  have  a  wheat  erop  from  six 
to  eight  bushels  per  acre  below  an  average, 
and  of  a  shrivelled  and  inferior  quality  • 
barley,  ten  bushels  below  an  average ;  oats' 
from  foui  to  six  bushels;  potatoes  ten  to 
twenty  per  cent,  above  an  average  and  less 
disease  by  fifty  per  cent,  than  last  year."  Ac 
cording  to  the  European  7 imes,  the  imports 
of  wheat  into  the  United  Kingdom  np  to 
he  end  ofJuly  was  4,513,821  quarters,  show- 
ing an  excess  of  about  3*  per  cent  over  the 
extraordinary  importation  of  the  correspond- 
ing period  of  last  year,  and  of  more  than  137 
per  cent,  over  those  of  the  same  month  of 
1860.    Since  the  end  of  July  the  arrivals 
from  America  have  continued  to  be  of  re- 
markable magnitude,  and  will  prepare  the 
country  to  meet  the  deficiency  of  the  har- 
vest crop.    The  cotton  famine  continues  to

bitn  received  no  satisfactory  reply.  The  pri- 
soner refused  to  give  any  account  of  himself,

be  bad  been  acquainted  with  him  long  enough 
to  know.  In  answer  to  the  Manistrate  be  said

against  bim.  he  was  discharged  with  a  caution. 

Edward  Collie,  a  notorious  character,  was 
charged  by  Edward  Walton  with  having 
maliciously  damaged  his  properly  and  being 
drnnk  and  disorderly.  The  prosecutor  said

drink,  which  be  refused.  As  the  prisoner  per- 
sisted in  attempting  to  enter  behind  the  coun-

ounces  weight  was  lying,  the  prosecutor  tried 
to  eject  him.  After  striking  at  bim  ineffectu- 
ally for  some  time,  the  prisoner  deliberately 
broke  a  large  square  of  glass  with  his  fist,  the 
amount  of  damage  done  being  valued  at  $35. 

The  prisoner  denied  all  kuowledge  of  the 
affair,  saying  that  he  had  tasted  no  food  since 
Sunday  morning,  and  tbut  a  drink  which  a 
friend  gave  him  had  doubtless  made  him  light- 
headed. A  very  bad  character  was  given  of 
him  by  the  police.  Mr.  Pemberton  said  that 
he  bad  given  bim  many  cautions,  and  bad 
eveu  interested  himself  to  provide  bim  em- 
ployment, of  which  opportunity  be  hud  not 
taken  advantage.  He  could  not  pass  over  this 
offence,  where  property  had  been  willfully  des- 
troyed that  ho  was  unable  to  pay  for.  He 
then  sentenced  bim  to  a  fine  of  £5,  out  ol

The  complainant  being  sworn,said  that  the 
prisoner  entered  his  saloon,  applying  to  him

tbe  counter ;  be  ordered  him  out,  upon  which

He  then  knocked  him  down, 'but  the  pris- 
oner getting  up  to  pursue  him,  he  ran  out  of 
the  house,  while  two  bystanders  restrained 
tbe  prisoner  from  further  violence.  The 
prisoner  afterwards 'had  threatened  to  lay  in 
wait  for  him,  and  had  been  seen  by  another 
lodger  to  load  a  revolver,  «o  that  he  (com- 
plainant) had  feared  to  return  to  the  house 
that  night,  believing  that  his  life  was  in 
danger. 

The  prisoner  pleaded  guilty,  but  said  that 
he  was  druuk  at  the  time  the  offence  was 
committed. 

The  Magistrate  accordingly  fined  him  £5 
for  the  assault,  and  ordered  him  to  furnish 
his  own  recognizance  of  £20,  and  two 
securities  in  £10  each,  to  keep  the  peace  for 
three  months.

Fire. — Yesterday  morning  some  alarm  was 
occasioned  in  tbe  immediate  neighborhood  by 
the  roof  of  a  dwelling  on  Broad  street  catching 
fire,  and  a  serious  conflagration  nearly  ensued. 
Tbe  cause  of  tbe  fire  was,  as  usual,  tbe  de- 
fective arrangemeut  of  a  stovepipe.  Much 
credit  is  due  to  tbe  prompt  action  of  Mr.  R. 
W.  Fawcett,  who,  tbe  moment  the  alarm  was 
raised,  climbed  up  one  of  the  supports  of  the 
verandah,  and  with  a  bucket  of  water,  which 
was  handed  up  to  bim,  extinguished  tbe 
flames. 

Temperance.— A  meeting  of  the  officers 
aud  members  of  tbe  Temperance  Society  re- 
cently formed  in  this  city  under  the  auspices 
of  the  Hon.  Malcolm  Cameron,  took  place  on 
Monday  evening,  and  was  well  attended- 
Considerable  business  was  transacted  in  tbe 
further  consolidation  of  this  much  needed 
movement.  The  name  of  this  association  is 
"The  Victoria  Total  Abstinence  Society," 
and  it  bids  fair  to  accomplish  much  gocd  in 
tbe  community.

Surgical. —  Mr.  Sweeney,  a  returned 
Cariboo  miner,  while  travelling  from  Antler 
to  Williams  Creek,  some  four  months  ago,  in 
crossing  a  log  on  the  trail,  slipped  his  foot 
and  fell,  wounding  as  he  thought  onlv  the 
muscles  of  the  inner  side  of  the  thigh. 
After  mnch  suffering  the  wound  closed, 
with  the  exception  of  an  orifice  large  enough 
to  introduce  a  smalt  probe.  The  case  was 
submitted  to  Dr.  Black  yesterday  and  after 
careful  examination  a  foreign  body  was  de- 
tected ;  and  after  a  skillfully  performed  ope- 
ration, six  piecos  of  wood,  tbe  largest  an 
inch  long,  and  half  an  inch  broad,  were 
found  imbedded  nearly  two  inches  deep  in 
the  muscles  of  the  inner  side  of  the  thigh. 
The  patient  is  doing  well.

Friday,  Oct.  24. 
Dr.  Walker,  a  dentist  residing  in  Fort 
street,  was  charged  as  insane  by  Sergeant 
Blake. 

The  Sergeant  said  that  he  had  found  the 

Erisoncr  between  the  hours  of  1  and  2  a.  m., 
nocking  at  the  door  of  a  cook  shop,  and 
shouting  at  the  top  of  his  voice  (like  Richard 
III),  "  A  horse !  a  horse  !  my  kingdom  for  a 
horse!"  Tho  officer  told  him  that  was  not  a 
likely  place  to  find  a  horse,  and  he  then  said  if 
he  could  but  find  a  midwife,  that  would  suit 
him  as  well.  Ho  believed  the  prisoner  to  be 
suffering  under  delirium  tremens,  brought  on 
by  habits  of  intemperance. 

Tbe  prisoner  said  he  was  very  sorry  to  be 
brought  again  before  the  bench  ;  be  had  a 
splendid  practice,  and  would  try  to  keep 
steady.   Even  now  he  only  drank  wine. 

Mr.  Pemberton  said  that  us  there  was  no 
lunntic  asylum  here,  ho  conld  not  entertain  the 
charge,  and  dismissed  the  prisoner  with  a 
recommendation  that  he  ehould  join  the  Hon. 
Malcolm  Cameron's  Temperance  Society. 

James  Walker  was  charged  by  special 
officer  Taylor  with  creating  a  disturbance  at 
an  untimely  hour,  and  damaging  private  prop- 
erty. Mr.  Taylor  said  he  had  seen  the  prisoner 
kick  some  barrels  off  tbe  sidewalk  iu  front  of 
Mr.  Bay  ley's  store,  into  the  road.  He  told 
bim  to  replace  them  and  go  away,  but  as  tbe 
prisoner  refused,  and  continued  disorderly,  bv 
arrested  bim.  Tbe  prisoner  then  endeavored 
to  escape,  and  frustrated  the  officer's  attempt 
to  blow  his  whistle  for  help.  He  wm  finally, 
with  assistance,  conveyed  to  iail. 

The  prisoner  represented  that  the  police  had 
used  unjustifiable  violence  in  taking  him  to 
the  lock  up,  and  several  witnesses  were  called

Water. — Some  time  since  we  mentioned 
that  a  scheme  for  bringing  fresh  water  from 
Spring  Vale,  Esquimau  Harbor,  tor  the  use 
of  the  Town  of  Victoria,  had  been  projected. 
A  reservoir  has  since  been  erected  upon 
Union  Wharf  capable  of  holding  30,000 
gallous,  and  a  vessel  by  which  the  fluid  is 
to  be  brought  from  the  springs,  having  on 
board  a  tank  of  40  tons  capacity,  built.  The 
water-boat  performed  her  first  trip  yester- 
day afternoon,  and  arrived  at  the  wharf 
about  5  o'clock.  The  hose  was  laid  on, 
and  the  contents  pumped  out  into  tbe  tank. 
We  learn  from  Mr.  Brodrick  that  water- 
carts  and  shipping  will  be  supplied  at  mod- 
erate rates,  so  soon  as  arrangements  are 
completed.  Iu  the  meantime  we  can  but 
hope  that  nothing  will  occur  to  mar  the 
consummation  of  this  most  useful  enter- 
prise. 

Naval  Intelligence.— II.  M.  S.  Mutine, 
Hecate,  and  Devastation,  and  the  gunboat 
Forward,   are  now    lying  at  Esqoimalt. 
Her  Majesty's   steam-frigate  Topaze,  51 
guns,  the  Hon.  Capt.  Spencer,  was  to  have 
left  Callao  on  the  9th  September.  The 
Royal  Naval  Hospital  has  been  removed 
from  Hospital  Point,  Esquimalt,  to  the  Boun- 
dary Commission  Barracks.   The  former 
locality  will  in  future  be  occupied  by  naval 
storehouses,  protected  by  fortifications.  The 
Grappler  steamed  into  Viotoria  Harbor  yM- 
terday  afternoon. 


Several  lucky  miners  who  came  down 
on  the  Eliza  Anderson  yesterday  had  heavy 
swags  of  the  precious  metal.  One  man 
step!  off  tho  boat  carrying  about  100  pounds 
of  dust,  snugly  done  up  in  canvas,  on  his 
shoulder,  looking  careful  at  the  gangway— 
which  happened  to  be  a  little  trail— lest  it 
should  give  way  with'  him  and  his  load. 
Five  or  six  others  had  neat  little  parcels  of 
50  or  60  pounds  each.  The  sightof  so  much 
gold  caused  several  of  the  bystanders  to  re- 
murk  that  they  would  give  Cariboo  still 
another  trial  next  year.

Enterprise  stuck  on  the  bar  going  up,  and 
was  delayed  several  hours. 

£?~An  alarm  of  fire  wm  raised  at  an 
early  hour  yesterday  morning,  smoke  having 
been  seen  issuing  from  the  roof  of  the 
Washington  Restaurant.  It  was  caused  by 
the  stove-pipe  becoming  overheated  by  too 
strong  a  fire,  but  fortunately  it  was  observed 
in  time  to  prevent  serious  consequences.

In  the  note  addressed  by  Count  Reichberg 
of  Austria.,  to  Count  Karolyi,  relative  to  the 
recognition  of  Italy  by  Prussia,  he  says  the 
Emperor  hopes  Prussia  may  never  have  to 
regret  the  step  she  has  taken,  and  as  to  the 
"  guarantees"  he  does  not  consider  they  are 
worth  the  paper  they  are  written  on. 

The  Prussian  Government  writing  to  its 
representatives  in  Germany,  explains  its 
views  on  tbe  projects  of  Federal  reform  pre- 
sented at  Frankfort  The  formation  of  a 
restricted  union  under  the  direction  of  a 
single  head,  both  as  regards  internal  and 
foreign  affairs,  is  strongly  advocated.  In 
another  circular  from  the  same  Government 
to  tbe  German  Cabinets,  the  reasons  which 
led  to  the  treaty  of  commerce  with  France 
are  explained,  and  it  is  declared  that  tbe 
governments  refusing  to  adbereto  it  have 
implicitly  severed  their  connection  with  the 
Zolverein.  Tbe  governments  already  known 
to  be  opposed  to  tbe  treaty  are  those  of  Wur- 
tenburg  and  Bavaria.  They  propose  a  con- 
ference at  Berlin  to  deliberate  concerning 
the  entry  of  Austria  into  the  Zolverein. 

Several  Prussian  papers  have  recently 
been  prosecuted  for  publishing  a  translation 
of  a  cutting  article  in  the  Saturday  Review, 
although  some  of  them  oontended  that  it 
was  only  to  rebut  tbe  allegations  contained 
in  it

A  political  meeting,  called  with  the  pro 
fesaed  object  of  obtaining  redress  for  certain 
political  grievances,  was  held  in  the  billiurd 
room  of  the  Colonial  Hotel  Saturday  evening. 
Between  100  and  150  persons  were  present, 
amongst  whom  there  might  have  been  40  resi- 
dent Colonists,  the  remainder  comprising  new- 
ly arrived  immigrants  Irom  England,  Canada, 
Austrulia  and  Cariboo. 

Mr.  Copland  having  been  called  to  the  chair, 
proceeded  to  explain  the  objects  in  view,  and  for 
which  the  meeting  had  been  auininoned.    He  al- 
luded to  the  existence  or  certain  well  known  griev- 
ances in  the  Colouy,  and  to  the  necessity  for  mak- 
ing these  known  to  the  Home  Goverununt.  Peti- 
tions had  been  repeatedly  sent,  but  bad  fulled  to 
accomplish  the  desired  end.    It  was  not  the  fault 
of  the  Imperial  Government  that  the  Colonists  had 
suffered  as  they  had  done.    It  was  the  people's 
fault,  that  they  had  not  applied  to  Her  Majesty  for 
redress.   The  Governor's  despatches  do  not  speak 
of  their  grievances.   The  Governor  liad  written 
that  the  people  of  British  Columbia  were  happy, 
when  he  (Mr.  Copland)  knew  very  well  they  were 
not  happy.   Some  say  the  Queen  does  not  oare ; 
but  she  would  oare,  if  she  heard  of  the  oppression 
of  her  subject*.   It  was  well  known  that  Her  Ma 
jeaty  was  a  most  motherly  woman,  and  that  she 
does  care  for  the  happiness  of  her  people.  The 
Governors  of  Colonies  were  in  the  position  of 
nurses  to  her  children,  and  it'  she  were  made  aware 
of  unhappiness  amongst  her  family,  her  first 
care  would  be  to  remove  the  cause.    It  was  no  use 
talking  of  grievances  in  the  streets  of  Victoria, 
their  grumbling  never  reached  farther.  If  they  laid 
the  matter  properly  before  Her  Majesty  they  would 
not  fail  to  obtain  redress.  • 

Mr.  Thorne  said  he  had  been  in  the  Colony 
many  years,  and  was  always  ready  to  assist  in  lay- 
ing its  grievances  before  the  throne.  He  then 
moved  the  following : 

It  is  Resolved  that  numerous  political  griev- 
ances exist  in  the  Colony  of  Vancouver  Island. 

Mr.  Earles  seconded  the  resolution  in  a  few 
words,  and  it  was  carried,  with  one  expression  of

RUSSIA. 

It  is  Raid  that  the  administrative  separation 
of  the  Kingdom  of  Poland  from  the  Bussian 
Empire  is  decided  on.   In  the  circular  note  of 
Prince  Gortscliakoft.  concerning  the  recogni- 
tion of  the  Kingdom  of  Italy  by  Buss  iu,  ho 
suys  :  "  It  is  not  only  questions  of  right  which 
are  under  consideration  ;  it  is  monarchial  prin- 
ciples and  sooial  order  contending  against  I 
revolutionary  anarchy.''    After  enumerating 
the  guarantees  given  by  Victor  Emanuel,  he 
adds  :  "  Under  these  circumstances,  we  hive 
thought  it  our  interest  to  maiuain  and  fortify 
the  Cabinet  of  Turin  on  the  ground  of  social 
order,  and  not  to  refuse  our  moral  support  to  a 
government  cbwen  by  the  enlightened  majority 
ol  a  country  towards  winch  we  entertain  good 
will  and  sympathy."    He  concludes  by  saying 
"The  Emperor  neither  raises  nor  resolves  the 
question  of  right."   A  company  has  been 
formed  at  Helsinglors  for  the  purpose  of  rais- 
iug  the  vessels  sunk  in  that  harbor  during  the 
Crimean  War.    Prince  Albrecht,  of  Prussia, 
who  holds  a  nominal  command  in  the  Bussian 
service,  as  chief  of  the  7th  regiment  of  Busaiun 
Cuirassiers,  is  about  to  leave  for  the  Caucasus, 
here-  hostilities  have  again  broken  out  , he-

that  there  were  people  in  thie 
country  who  owned  property,  and  were  un-

speculation— and  desired  a  law  to  rectify  it ;

The  speakers  were  all  well-intentioned 
men  ;  but  it  is  an  orthodox  saying  that  '  hell 
is  paved  with  good  intentions."  So  we  think 
in  this  instance  the  intentions  of  all  the 
movers  of  the  «  monster  meeting"  were

exposed  their  own  weakness.    They  were 
not  even  supported  by  the  coantenance  of

ble  people,  men  of  substance,  spectator*,

corner  way,  would  be  acceptable  to  the 
country ;  but  Mr.  McClure,  never.  Such 
were  and  nre  public  comments,  to  which  we

delegate  can  now  rejoice,  snap  his  fingers

cillors, and  proceed  by  the  next  steamer.  A

Downing  street  in  advance,  so  that  a  fleet  | 
may  be  sent  to  cruise  oil  the  chops  of  the 
Channel  to  welcome  the  delegute.     After  I 
that  irwill  be  Sir  Leonard. 

As  very  few  men  at  so  early  nn  age  have 
had  such  an  important  mission  confided  to

pendent electors,  it  may  not  be  out  of  place

have  been  born  ;  or  even  if  he  never  was  born.

TURKEY. 

The  Montenegrin  war  is  at  length  ter- 
minated, the.  Turks  having  taken  Cetigne. 
Prince  Nicolas  and  AJirko  with  the  Mon- 
tenegrins fled  Into  the  Austrian  territory 
after  setting  fire  to  the  principal  buildings  in 
the  town.  All  the  battles  betweon  the  two 
parties  were  most  desperately  contested  on 
the  part  of  me  mountaineer*  ;  women  and 
children,  old  and  young,  all  taking  their 
part  and  fighting  with  the  utmost  determi- 
nation. The  Italians  now  visiting  Turkey 
have  been  very  .warmly  received  by  the 
Sultan  and  his  chief  officers. 

From  Greece  «e  learn  that  the  present 
ministry  is  very  unpopular.  The  Court  still 
persists  in  the  dangerous  policy  of  refusing 
an  amnesty  for  the  late  disturbance.^  It  fij 
thought  that  the  organisation  of  the  National 
Guard  will  take  place  only  on  paper,  as  the 
Treasury  is  empty.

Mr.  Cary  again  said  it  was  most  objectionable. 
There  was  /eally  no  danger  to  be  apprehended 
Irom  the  erection  of  the  magazine  inside  the  nor-,

the  proper  officer,  who  had  assured  them  that  no 
fit  place  for  the  purpose  existed  outside  of  Laurel 
Point.  • 

Mr.  Tolmio  thought  that  other  people  than  the 
Executive  had  a  right  to  hold  an  opinion.  Official 
wisdom  was  not  always  the  best.  Mauy  persons 
objected  to  the  magaxine  being  built  outside  the 
harbor. 

Mr.  Cary  said  that  if  the  amendment  were 
adopted,  the  result  would  be  no  magazine  at  all. 
As  the  Executive  had  decided  there  was  no  fit 
place  outside  the  harbor,  it  would  be  asking  them 
to  act  contrary  to  conviction.  Naval  officers 
laughed  at  the  idea  of  putting  a  magazine  in  a 
place  where  it  might  most  easily  be  reached  by  an 
enemy.  Members  must  recollect  that  they  had 
very  unsettled  neighbors. 

Mr.  Tolmie  said  that  the  proper  place  for  the 
powder  in  time  of  war  was  Esquimau,  where 
would  be  under  the  protection  of  the  fleet  ai 
fortifications.  .  . 

Mr.  Cary  agreed,  but  suggested  that  troubles 
sometimes  arose  very  unexpectedly.  Moreover, 
placing  the  powder  in  such  an  unguarded  situation, 
would  be  offering  a  premium  to  dishonest  persons 
who  might  walk  off  with  the  whole  any  dark  night 
they  thought  proper.  . 

Mr.  Tolmie  thought  the  change  of  situation 
would  not  tend  to  its  security,  and  should  anyone 
maliciously  blow  it  up  life  and  jrroparty  would  be 
safe. 





Mr.  Cary  considered  that  in  a  I 
situation,  no  one  would  have  the  audacity  t 
such  an  attempt.  If  the  amendment  were  adopted, 
he  felt  sure  that  the  Governor's  assent  would  not 
be  accorded,  and  the  bill  would  be  valueless. 
The  amendment  was  then  put  and  carried. 
The  amendment  proposing  to  change  the  price 
of  storage  Irom  $3  to  $4  per  month  was  passed 
over,  as  the  members  agreed  that  it  was  an  in- 
fringement of  the  privileges  of  the  House  for  the 
Council  to  suggest  such  alterations. 

Several  unimportant  clerical  amendments  were 
then  considered,  and  the  bill  reported  complete. 

Progress  was  reported  upon  the  Stamp  Act, 
when  the  House  adjourned  until  Tuesday  nestf, 
at  7  p-  m.

Gale  IN  Tti k  Sound.— It  blew  quite  a 
gale  in  tho  Sound  last  Thursday  evening. 
The  sloop  Midnight  Cry  was  dismasted,  but 
succeeded  in  getting  safely  into  Port  Angelos. 
The  Winged  Bacer,  which  took  refuge  in 
this  harbor, reports  that  she  met  an  outward 
bound  ship,  supposed  to  be  the  Mountain 
Wave,  with  her  fore  top-sail  split,  main  top- 
sail sheet  carried  away,  and  several  other  of 
her  anils  flying;  but  it  was  blowing  so  bard 
that  Capt.  Smart  could  not  note  exactly  the 
amount  of  damage  done.

Cowiciian.— The  schoouer  Trader  arrived 
from  Cowichan  last  Friday  night.  Every- 
thing is  going  on  smoothly  in  the  settlement, 
the  settlers  being  all  busy  building  houses  and 
making  other  preparations  for  the  approaching 
winter.  The  Indians  are  quiet  and  friendly, 
seeming  to  be  very  well  disposed  towards  their 
white  neighbors.  The  Trader  will  sail  again 
for  the  settlement  ou  Tuesday  next. 


Sloop  Lost. — The  sloop  Wild  Duck  was 
driven  ashore  on  the  spit  near  Port  Angelos 
last  Wednesday  night  and  became  a  total 
wreck.  There  is  a  heavy  surf  always  break- 
ing over  the  spit,  even  in  calm  weather,  and 
as  it  was  blowing  hard  at  tho  time  she  was 
soon  oompletely  broken  up.  Tho  crew  got 
safely  to  shore.

Post  Office 

Editoe  Bbitish 
read  many  communications  in  the  }ournals  setting 
forth  in  strong  and  justifiable   terms  the  dis 
graceful  manner  in  which  the  business  of  the 
Postal  Department  of  this  Colony  is  carried  on.  1 
can  no  longer  refrain  from  writing  a  few  lines  on 
the  subject.    What  it  the  use  of  having  a  Post- 
master General  who  orders  the  Postmasters  at  the 
different  stations  'not  to  allow  any  single  mail  to 
leave  their  office  for  the  upper  country  exceeding 
twelve  pounds  in  weight  ?'  Thus  many  letters  must 
Uy  over,  and  no  newspapers  forwarded,  or  very 
few.    Letters  from  Victoria  to  this  place  are  now 
frequently  fifteen  davs  and  more  reaching  Lillooet, 
I  myself  received  yesterday  two  letters  from  Vic- 
toria, one  dated  Sept.  19th,  and  the  other  Sept. 
23d,  thus  the  formei  was  nineteen  and  the  latter 
fifteen  days  on  the  road.   The  letter  dated  on  the 
19th  was  sent  by  express,  which  I  received  nine- 
teen days  after  its  Victoria  date.   There  is  no  de- 
pending on  letters  put  in  the  post  office  reaching 
Victoria  from  this  place  in  any  reasonable  time  ; 
and  every  one  who  wishes  for  speedy  and  sure 
transit  of  their  letters  has  to  put  them  in  Ballon  s 
Express  Office,  which  forwards  them  in  from  five 
to  eight  days  the  same  distance  that  the  Royal 
Mail  takes  about  fifteen  days.    A  friend  of  mine 
had  letter*  up  yesterday  by  Ballon'*  Express  dated 
Victoria,  Oct.  1st.   It  is  too  bad  that  proper 
measures  are  not  taken  to  put  the  mail  service  on 

a  better  footing.  .    .   . 

The  above  are  not  the  only  instances  I  could 
name ;  but  I  have  frequently  observed  the  slow 
and  slovenly  manner  iu  which  the  post  office  for- 
wards the  mails.  It  is  a  disgrace  to  any  British 

I  am,  Sir, 
Your  obedient  servant, 

A.  G.  SELWYN. 

Lillooet,  October  9th.

Antler  Crebk.— The  rumors  in  regard 
to  the  abandonment  of  Antlor  Creek  before 
the  close  of  tho  mining  season  prove  to  have 
been  quite  unfounded.   Several  claims  have 
yielded  richly  during  the  whole  summer, 
and  many  others  have  paid  excellent  wages. 
The  Smith  &  Hazetino  claim  has  cleaned 
upwards  of  87,000  to  the  share.  The  Murray 
claim  has  also  turned  out  a  largo  snm,  Ire- 
quently  washing  up  thirty  to  fifty  ouncesper 
day  towards  the  olose  of  the  season.  Two 
or  three  others  are  also  spoken  of  as  having 
paid  several  thousand  dollars  each.  There 
will  be  no  men  wintering  on  Antler,  with  the 
exception  of  a  tow  who  will  bo  left  to  watch 
the  claims,  as  tho  diggings  on  this  creek  are 
too  shallow  to  allow  work  to  be  oarried  on 
during  the  winter  months.

paired at  Steilaooom,  and  is  now  daily  ex 
pooled  to  arrivo  at  Port  Angoloo.

The  Late  Simon  Fearer,  Esq.,  the  Discov- 
rkrr  ov  Fraber  BivER-The  death  of  Mr. 
Fraser,  of  St.  Andrews,  and  of  Mrs.  Fraser,  a 
few  hours  after  her  husband's  decease,  baa 
been  already  briefly  recorded  iu  these  columns, 
but  a  more  extended  notice  is  due  to  this 
worthy  and  respected  gentleman.   On  Thurs- 
day, the  21st  alt.,  the  earthly  remains  ot  the 
departed  pair  were  placed  in  the  burying 
ground  of  St.  Andrews,  in  the  presence  of  a 
large  assemblage  of  their  friends  and  neigh- 
bors   Ono  grave  received  both  coffins ;  the 
bodies  were  laid  aide  by  side  ;  united  in  life, 
nnd  not  oven  in  death  divided.  In  Mr.  Fraser 
the  country  loses  not  only  one  of  its  moat  re- 
Rpcctablo  and  honored  residents,  but  one  of 
the  most  illustrious  men  that  ever  settled 
within  its  borders.   One  of  the  few  survivors 
of  the  fine  old  "  North- Westers,"  Mr.  Fraser'a 
name  as  the  discoverer  and  first  explorer  of 
the  gold  stream  which  bears  hia  name,  will  be 
remembered  with  honor  long  after  the  moat  of 
bis  Provincial  cotemporariea  have  been  for- 
gotten.  Mr.  Fraser  was  the  youngest  son  of 
Mr.  Simon  Fraser,  who  emigrated  to  the 
State  of  New  York  in  1773.   He  purchased 
land  near  Bennington ;  but  upon  the  breaking 
out  of  the  Bevolutionary  war,  he  attached 
himself  to  the  royal  cause,  and  served  as  cap- 
tain  at  the  battle  of  Bennington,  where  he  was-* 
captured  by  tho  rebels.   He  died  io  Albany 
jail  about  13  months  afterwards,  hia  end  being 
hastened  by  the  rigorous  nature  of  his  im- 
prisonment. He  was  married  to  Isabella  Grant, 
daughter  of  Daldregan,  and  had  issue,  four 
tons  and  five  daughters.   The  widow  with  het* 
children  came  to  Canada  after  the  peace  of 
1783.— Cornwall  Freeholder

Our  Currency. 


We  have  noticed  the  prevalence  in  this 
Colony  of  a  practice  which  might  possibly  be 
productive  of  some  inconvenience ;  we  allude 
to  the  fact  that  on  a  great  many  contracts 
made  iu  this  place  the  consideration  money  is 
specified  to  bo  paid  in  dollars  and  cents,  of 
U.  States  currency.  Practically,  dollars  and 
oenta  arc  oar  principal  denomination  of  money; 
io  fact,  utmost 


known  amongst  us.  Yet  we  are  not  aware 
that  any  legal  enactment  has  ever  positively 
declared  or  recognized  the  dollar  to  be  a  de- 
nomination of  our  money,  or  specified  its  value. 
As  the  dollar  is  the  name  given  in  several 
countries  te  a  certain  description  of  money, 
and  as  the  value  in  each  place  varies  some- 
what, we  are  consequently  driven  to  the  expe- 
dient, wheu  we  make  use  of  the  terra  in  legal 
documents,  of  particularizing  what  we  mean 
by  it.  If  we  speak  of  American  currency,  is 
there  not  tome  danger  under  existing  circum- 
stances of  giving  rise  to  disputes  ?  Ten  dollars 
in  American  currency  may  now  mean  a  Treas- 
ury Note  for  that  sum,  since  they  have  been 
made  a  legal  tender  in  the  States.  Of  course 
no  one  here  ever  contemplated  being  paid  a 
debt  in  United  States  paper  ;  yet  is  there  not 
a  possibility  of  such  a  contingency,  where  a 
contract  specifies  tlie  payment  of  a  certain  sum 
in  American  currency  f   Some  have  provided

Returned. — Father  D'Herbonez,  the 
Superior  of  the  Oblate  Fathers,  in  this  island 
and  British  Columbia,  and  who  now  resides 

at  his  mission  at  Esquimalt,  recently  re- 
turned on  the  Sierra  Nevada  from  France 
with  three  Oblate  Fathers  and  two  lay 
Brothers.  The  object  of  Father  O'Herbonez 
in  going  to  France  was  the  election  of  a  Su- 
perior over  the  Order  throughout  the  world. 
The  names  of  the  recently  arrived  Fathers 
are  Gendre,  Boudre,  and  LeJao.  The  order 
appears  to  be  prospering  in  these  provinces, 
but  chiefly  among  the  native  tribes. 


Coroker's  Imqbmt.— An  inquest  was  held 
last  Saturday  iu  the  Boomerang  Inn,  over  the 
body  of  a  man  named  Alexander  Gray,  a  re- 
turned Stickeen  adventurer,  who  was  fouud 
dead  in  his  teut  on  Friday  night.  The  evi- 
dence bhowed  that  since  bis  return  from  Stick- 
een, he  bad  taken  to  excessive  drinking,  hav- 
ing been  constantly  drunk  for  nearly  ten  days, 
und  from  having  taken  no  nourishment,  he. was 
in  »  state  of  great  exhaustion.  On  the  night 
of  his  death,  be  staggered  out  of  his  teut  to 
where  his  comrades  were  Bitting  around  a  fire, 
and  was  helped,  back  again,  where  he  must, 
have  almost  immediately  expired,  as,  when 
they  entered,  a  lew  minutes  afterwards,  life  was 
extinct.  Dr.  Lacy,  who  attended  him.  was  of 
the  opinion,  with 'the  Coroner,  that  bis  death 
was  caused  by  apoplexy,  brought  on  by  ex- 
cessive drinking  ;  und  the  jury  brought  in  a 
verdict  accordingly.  Deceased  had  about  850 
in  the  hands  of  a  comrade,  which  was  taken 
possession  of  by  officer  Crowley. 

Suspicious.— Capt.  Mitchell,  of  the  Trader, 
on"  his  last  return  trip,  passed  a  canoe,  manned 
by  five  Indians  calling  themselves  Fort  Rupert 
Indians,  which  had  evidently  belonged  to  white 
men,  as  it  was  rigged  with  mast  and  Bail,  oars, 
etc.  His  suspicions  were  aroused  by  learning 
that  tbey  had  been  endeavoring  to  sell  it  at 
several  places,  and  by  the  fact  that  they  did 
not  give  a  straightforward  accouut  of  how  it 
came  into  their  possession.  It  may,  however, 
have  been  sold  to  them  by  some  returning 
Stickeenites,  who  came  down  in  some  other 

craft.  

Alleged  Rape. — Two  men  have  been 
locked  up  on  a  charge  of  rape ;  one  being 
arrested  in  town,  the  other  out  at  Saanich. 
The  crime  said  to  have  been  committed  oc- 
curred at  Saanich,  and  the  complainants  ure 


of  the  obligations  of  an  oath,  replied,  through 
her  interpreter,  that  she  believed  in  the 
truths  of  Christianity  ;  she  had  been  baptized 
by  a  priest  who  had  given  her  her  first  name  ; 
and  she  knew  that  it  she  did  right  she  would 
go  to  heaven  ;  if  wrong,  that  she  would  go 
to  hell.  The  two  complainants  were  heard, 
and  tbey  seemed  to  stand  crocs-examination 
very  well.  They  both  asserted  that  they 
were  faithful  and  sober  wives,  but  confessed 
to  having  imbibed  a  few  glasses  of  liquor 
before  the  occurrence  which  led  to  thin  trial. 
The  only  white  witness,  George  Fea,  testified

The  Inter-Colonial  Railway. 

By  our  late  exchanges  from  the  Eastern 
Provinces  we  learn  that  the  inter  colonial 
Convention  appointed  from  Nova  Scotia, 
New  Brunswick  and  Canada  to  conferon  the 
subject  of  an  Inter-Colonial  Railroad,  had 
just  terminated  their  sittings  most  satisfac- 
torily. The  Convention  consisted  of  the  Gov- 
ernors of  the  three  PiovInces,with  delegations 
of  their  respective  ministers,  so  that  we  may 
conclude  that  every  justice  was  done  towards 
the  object  of  their  meoting.  As  far  as  we 
are  yet  able  to  learn  of  the  results  of  their 
labors  it  would  seem  that  Canada  is  to 
bear  five-twelfths,  and  New  Brunswick  and 
Nova  Scotia,  between  them,  seven-twelfths 
of  the  amount  of  the  expenses  of  building 
the  road.  According  to  this  Canada  would 
have  to  pay  some  £50,000  a  year — the  whole 
amount  being  £120,000  yearly  interest  on 
the  capital  required  to  build  the  line.  The 
three  Colonies  will  havo  to  raise  the  money 
required  for  the  work:  but  as  the  imperial 
Government  has  agreed  to  become  their 
security,  they  will  have  plenty  of  offers  for 
all  they  want.   It  only  remains  for  the  re- 


the  new  arrangements,  the  next  meeting  will  mii0^  for  0,)0  fc. 
take  place  to-uiorrow  evening,  at  7  o'clock.

New  Building.— The  erection  of  a  stone 
building  on  the  west  side  of  Store  street,  and 
in  the  ravine,  was  commenced  last  week. 


New  Shears. — A  couple  of  smut  spars  are 
being  got  ready  for  erection  upon  the  Hudson 
Bay  Company's  wharf,  for  masting  purposes. 


U.  S.  Soldiers  in  the  City.— Several 
American  soldiers  visited  our  city  yester- 
day. They  belong  to  company  C,  9th  regi- 
ment of  U.S.  Infantry,  under  the  command 
of  Capt.  Bissell,  which  is  stationed  at  the  mil- 
itary post  of  occupation  on  San  Juan  Island. 
The  Company  have  not,  till  now,  received 
pay  for  six  months, — some  of  them  not  for ' 
sixteen  months,  bavins  been  out  on  a  Gov- 
ernment expedition.  They  were  all  paid  in 
Treasury  notes,  which  are  quite  current 
across  the  Sound,  but  when  the  men  came 
to  Victoria,  to  their  surprise  nobody  would 
look  at  '.heir  nhmplastera.  They  ultimately 
got  them  cashed  at  the  heavy  shave  of  twenty 
per  cent.

For  the  North. — The  sloop  Leonede, 
Capt.  McAlmond,  sails  this  morning  with  10 
tons  of  freight  for  Bentinck  Arm,  and  two 
spective  colonial  legislatures  to  give  their  I  tona  of  freight  and  one  passenger  for  Queen 
assent  to  the  arrangements  entered  into  by 
their  ministers  to  have  the  work  commenced 
as  soon  as  the  Imperial  Government  have 
signified  where  the  line  is  to  run.  This,  we 
believe,  was  one  of  the  conditions  of  the  Im- 
perial guarantee  being  given.  The  whole 
length  of  the  road  is  estimated  at  400  miles, 
and  iU  cost  at  about  £4,000,000.  When 
completed  there  will  be  an  uninterupted 
rail  communication  between  Halifax,  on  tbo 
Atlantic,  and  Windsor,  opposite  Detroit,  in 
Michigan,  all  in  British  territory.  A  mag- 
nificent instalment  of  the  great  iron  belt 
which  is  toencirlo  the  continent.  Every  day 
seems  to  hasten  the  movement  of  those  cir- 
cumstauses  which  are  to  aid  in  connecting 
the  two  oceans. 

The  Canadian  Government  have  jn9t  author- 
ized the  construction  of  a  roud  from  Colling- 
wood,  which  is  the  northern  terminus  of  rail- 
way communication,  in  Canada,  up  along  the 
east  coast  of  Georgian  Bay  to  the  Bruco  cop- 
per mines,  near  the  function  of  Lakes  Huron 
and  Superior.  This  will  have  tho  effect  of 
throwing  several  buudrcd  miles  open  to  settle- 
ment, and  paving  the  way  for  railway  com- 
munication. From  Lake  Superior  to  the 
plains  of  the  Saskatchewan  is  but  a  step  which 
could  soon  be  spanned  by  a  road  from  thence 
to  the  base  of  the  Rocky  Mountains.  No 
doubt  the  Home  Government  will  contribute 
liberally,  as  that  would  be  a  work  of  Imperial 
interest.  We,  on  our  parts,  must  be  prepared 
to  meet  them  at  the  great  dividing  line,  and 
thns  cap  the  climax  to  this  magnificent  trans- 
continental communication.

Thomas  Casey,  a  boatman,  was  charged 
by  officer  Shepherd  with  creating  a  disturb- 
ance an  Saturday  night,  between  9  and  10 
o'clock,  on  Little's  Wharf,  with  some 
drunken  Indians. 

In  answer  to  a  question  from  the  bench, 
the  officer  said  that  these  Indians  were  in 
the  employ  of  Mr.  Little  during  the  day,  and 
constantly  drunk  at  night. 

Mr.  Pemberton  said  that  it  was  Mr.  Little 
who  should  be  indicted.  Nothing  being 
known  against  the  prisoner's  oharacter  ho 
was  discharged. 

Several  men  looked  up  for  being  drunk 
and  disorderly  were  sentenced  to  the  usual 
fine  of  five  shillings.

terday  morning  with  having  committed  the 
above  outrage  upon  the  persons  of  Mrs.

boorie,  both  Indian  women  legally  married.

Arrival. — The  bark  Adelaide  Cooper  ar- 
rived in  this  port  yesterday  evening  from  San 
Francisco,  having  been  twenty-seven  days 
on  the  way.  She  had  light  head  winds  dur- 
ing the  whole  voyage.  The  barkentinc  Fre- 
mont was  lying  off  Race  Rocks  drifting  with 
the  tide,  when  the  Adelaide  Cooper  oame  in. 
The  Fremont  has  80  head  of  cattle  on  board, 
but  had  not  lost  one,  although 'twenty -six 
days  out.  

Aid  to  Lancashire  Operatives. — Tho 
great  suffering  ol  the  working  classes  in 
Lancashire  has  given  rise  to  a  great  deal  of 
sympathy  both  at  home  and  in  the  colonies; 
and  efforts  have  been  made  in  various  places 
to  help  to  alleviate  the  distress  of  tho  poor 
sufferers  by  means  of  contributions  for  that 
purpose.  We  are  glad  to  learn  that  a  simi- 
lar movement  is  spoken  of  in  town,  on  be- 
half of  this  Colony.  It  is  to  be  hoped  that  it 
will  meet  with  cvory  encouragement. 


Oyster  Trade.  —  Our  remarks  on  this 
branch  of  trade,  mode  in  the  Colonist  n  few 
days  ago,  have  not  been  without  their  effect ; 
the  aloop  Eagle  has  been  fitted  out  with  oyster 
rakes,  etc,,  and  sailed  yesterday  for  Salt 
Spring  Island,  to  take  in  a  load  of  the  deli- 
cious bivalves.  Should  the  adventure  prove 
profitable,  doubtless  others  will  be  induced  to 
go  into  the  business,  which  may  thus,  ere  long, 
assume  respectable  dimensions.

In  the  appeal  case  of  Vassel.Miot  and  others

The  French  Agricultural  and  Commercial 
journals  say  that  on  the  whole  the  harvest 
of  1862  may  be  considered  that  of  an  average 
year,  that  is  to  say,  about  05,000,000  of  hec- 
tolitres, or  nearly  the  annual  consumption  of 
France.  The  wine  crop  it  is  expected  will 
be  more  than  the  average. 

The  Emperor  lately  attended  the  Polygon 
of  Vincemies  to  witness  experiments  with 
new  war  material.  Cartridges  wuh  com- 
pressed powder,  a  new  infantry  musket,  an 
invention  called  the  '-  pendolem  cannon," 
and  a  new  system  of  breecb-loading  gun 
were  tried  before  his  Majesty  with  satisfac- 
tory results. 

Marshal  Canrobert,  in  addressing  the 
troops  upon  the  breaking  up  of  the  camp  at 
Chalons,  says  that  the  infantry,  cavalry  and 
artillery  are  equal  to  any  thing  that  the 
country  and  Emperor  may  demand  from 
their  devotedness. 

M.  Michal  Chevalier  in  an  address  to  the 
Council- General  of  Herault,  of  which  he  is 
the  President,  dwells  very  strongly  on  the 
hospitable  manner  in  which  the  English 
Government  and  English  society  received 
visitors  to  the  International  Exhibition.

soring the  acts  of  a  Judge  of  Instruction  on

not  proceeded  in.

No  Money.  No  Justice. 

We  were  always  oi  the  idea  that  tbe 
administration  of  justice  was  an  object  of  the ' 
highest  importance  to  the  public,  and  that  the 
question  of  its  cost  was  one  of  a  secondary 
nature.  It  would  seem,  however,  that  our 
Police  Commissioner  entertains  quite  a  differ- 
ent opinion  on  the  subject.  It  one  were  to 
judge  from  appearatlccs,  one  would  say  that 
Mr.  IVmbcrtuu's  idea  is  that  he  is  not  obliged 
to  entertain  any  charge  of  a  criminal  nature 
ftRaiust  an  offending  individual  unless  the  ex- 
penses be  guaranteed  before  hand.  Uis  motto 
would  seem  to  be  '•  no  trust  here  !*'  V  Let  jus- 
tice be  done,  though  the  heavens  should  fall," 
was  the  saying  of  a  period  less  enlightened 
than  ours.  It  would  seem,  however,  that  ns 
the  worla  grows  older  and  wiser,  that  maxim 
can  no  longer  be  entertained.  In  some  coun- 
tries tbey  are  still  foolish  enough  to  adhere  to 
the  old  idta  ;  where,  if  a  man  is  charged  with 
a  crime,  he  is  compelled  to  appear  and  answer 
for  it,  be  the  complainant  rich  or  poor.  No 
criminal  is  suffered  to  escape  through  the  in- 
ability of  tbe  prosecutor  to  pay  coats.  There 
the  strong  arm  of  the  law  does  not  require 
dollars  and  cents  to  put  it  iu  motion ;  the 
public  pay  lor  the  administration  of  justice, 
and  they  leave  it  free  to  all  it  necessary. 
Here,  however,  a  man  may  be  kicked  and 
cuffed  by  any  brutal  ruffian,  and  if  he  is  too 
poor  to  pay  tbe  price,  of  a  summons  or  war- 
rant,  he  has  no  redress.  Is  it  for  that  the 
Colony  pays  to  support  a  stipendiary  magis- 
trate '  Is  that  institution  a  speculation  which 

must  Ha  maila  tn  luv  at  all  nvsnta  1  Ta  flu* 
jjiusb  uo  umuu   icf  pav  ui>  an  ociiia  r     is  lire 

miserable  style  of  false  economy  which  per- 
vades our  mail  system  to  be  extended  to  tbe 

administration  of  justice  ?  We  hope  we 
shall  not  have  to  chronicle  another  instance 
of  the  denial  of  redress  such  as  that  mentioned 
in  our  local  columns  to-day.  We  expect  some- 
thing  better  in  a  British  Colony. 

Peace  River. —  A  professional  gentleman 


of  this  city,  recently  from  Fort  Alexandria, 
whilst  on  his  way  down  from  Lightning 
Creek,  met  at  the  former  place  a  party  of 
miners  who  were  buying  provisions  to  re- 
turn to  Peace  River,  where  they  had  been 
working  all  summer.  One  of  the  party  in- 
formed him  that  "Peace  River  and  the  whole 
upper  country  is  just  the  place  for  the 
miners."  The  diggings  were  principally 
bar  diggings,  and  the  gold  very  line — pay- 
ing from  $30  to  SloO  a  day  with  a  single 
rocker,  without  quicksilver.  He  infoiius  us 
that  with  quicksilver  it  would  double.  Tbe 
Peace  River  party  asserted  that  there  was 
room  for  20,000  miners,  with  the  advantage 
of  working  a  longer  season  and  a  milder 
climate  than  Caribou. 

DaowNED^ilrrBar^eldrindian  Agent 
at  Barclay  Sound,  and  well  known  for  many 
years  in  Victoria,  was  unfortunately  drowned 
in  the  Sound  last  Monday  afternoon.  lie 
had  gone  out  in  a  canoe  with  an  Indian  to 
meet  the  schooner  Alberni,  which  was  ex- 
pected in,  and  was  never  again  seen.  Tbe 
Indian  returned  with  bis  canoe  half  full  of 
water,  and  stated  that  .Mr.  Banfield  had  in- 
cautiously  leant  to  one  side  of  the  canoe  and 
upset  it,  and  that  he  sank  immediately.  It 
is  thought  rather  singular  that  Mr.  B.,  who 
was  quite  at  home  in  a  canoe,  should  have 
been  upset  so  simply  :  but  it  is  impossible 
to  judge  of  the  correctness  of  the  account,  as 
the  Indian  was  tbe  only  witness  to  the  sad 
accident.  . 

Tale  Roads.— While  the  Governor  was  at 
Yale,  a  proposition  was  made  to  him  to  have 
a  road  constructed  from  Emory's  Bar  to  Yale, 
a  distance  of  four  miles.  Tbe  Governor  was 
favorably  impressed  with  tbe  project ;  and  it 

is  supposed  that  a  call  will  be  made  for  ten- 
ders to  complete  it.  The  rock  at  Sawmill 
Riffle  is  to  be  blasted.  In  three  wetks  the 
road  around  tbe  bluff' above  Yale  will  be  com- 
pleted. When  tbe  contracts  of  Trutcb  aod 
of  Spence  and  a  suspension  bridge  across  the 
Fraser  2  miles  above  the  ferry,  when  all  are  com- 
pleted, as  they  will  be  by  next  spring,  a  wagon

An  Act  to  Prohibit  Sunday  Trading. 

Whereas  it  it  the  practice  in  all  Her  Majesty's 
dominions  to  observe  and  keep  holy  the  Lord's 
Day,  commonly  called  Sunday ;  and  it  is  expe- 
dient to  ensure  the  better  observance  of  the  same 
in  this  Colony  : 

Be  it  therefore  enacted,  Ac. :  That  no  trades 
man,  artificer,  workman,  laborer,  or  other  persons 
whatsoever,  shall  do  or  exercise  any  worldly  la- 
bor, business,  or  work  of  his  ordinary  calling  apon 
the  Lord's  Day,  or  any  part  thereof  (works  of  ne- 
cessity and  charity  excepted) ;  and  that  every  per- 
son of  the  age  of  fourteen  years  or  upwards  offend- 
remise.,  shall  for  every  such  offence 
am  of  five  pounds  (£5)  to  be  recovered 
ry  way  before  any  Justice  of  the  Peace. 
That  no  person  or  persons  whatsoever  shall  pub- 
licly cry,  shew  forth,  or  expose  for  sale,  any  ware, 
merchandise,  fruits,  vegetables,  goods  or  chattels, 
whatsoever  upon  the  Lord's  Day,  or  any  part 
thereof,  upon  pain  that  any  such  person  so  offend- 
ing shall  forfeit  the  same  goods  so  cried,  shown 
forth,  or  exposed  for  sale. 

Provided,  that  nothing  in  this  Act  shall  apply  to 
Apothecaries  and  chemists,  nor  to  the  vendors  of

The  fighting  on  Saturday  is  described  as  des-

bably an  over  estimate.

At  Archidelphia,  50  miles  southwest  of  Little 
Rock,  the  rebels  manufacture  munitions  of  war, 
and  have  removed  the  State  papers  and  records 
there.  The  rebel  officers  boast  of  their  intention 
to  Invade  Missouri. 

Washington,  October  8.— Gen.  McClellan,  !n 
calling  the  attention  of  the  officers  and  soldiers  of 
the  army  of  the  Potomace  to  tbe  General  Order 
published  in  the  array  on  the  President's  Emanci- 
pation Proclamation,  says  :  The  constitution 
confides  to  the  civil  authority  the  power  of  making, 
expounding  and  executing  the  laws.  Armed  forces 
are  raised  and  supported  simply  to  sustain  the  au- 
thorities, and  are  to  be  held  in  atrict  subordination 
thereto.  In  all  respects  this  fundamental  rule  of 
our  political  system  is  essectial  to  the  security  of 
our  republican  institutions,  and  should  be  thor- 
oughly understood  and  observed  by  every  soldier. 
The  principle  upon  whioh,  and  objects  for  which, 
the  army  shall  bo  employed  in  suppressing  the 
rebellion,  must  be  determined  and  declared  by 
civil  authority,  thereforo  the  Executive  is  the 
proper  and  only  source  through  which  the  views 
and  orders  oi  Government  can  be  laid  down  to 
armies.  Discussion  by  officers,  concerning  public 
measures  determined  upon  by  Government,  is 
adverted  to  by  Gen.  McClellan  as  an  evil  against 
which  it  is  thought  advisablo  to  guard  the  armies 
of  the  republic,  as  it  tends  greatly  to  impair  and 
destroy  the  discipline  of  the  troops  by  substituting 
a  spirit  of  political  faction  for  that  firm,  steady, 
and  earnest  support  of  the  Government,  whioh  is 
the  highest  duty  of  the  soldier. 

St.  Louis,  Oct  8.— Col.  Rod  Matheson  died

Major  S.  S.  Graham,  a  San  Francisco  politician,

heavier  blow  on  our  credit  than  the  public  euemy.

Washington,  Oct.  10.— General  McCleBan's 
congratulatory  order  to  the  army  says :  We  de- 
feated a  numerous  and  powerful  army  of  the 
enemy,  in  an  action  that  was  desperately  fought, 
and  remarkable  for  duration  and  the  destruction 
of  life  which  attend  d  it.  The  obstinate  bravery 
of  the  troops  of  Hooker,  Mansfield,  and  Sumner, 
the  daring  gallantry  of  rraukltn'a  corps,  the 
steady  valor  of  Burnside's  troops,  present  a  bril- 
liant spectacle  to  our  countrymen,  which  will  swell 
their  hearts  with  pride  snd  exultation.  14  guns, 
39  colors,  15,500  small  arms,  and  6,000  prisoners, 
are  evidence  of  the  completeness  of  our  triumph. 

The  Guerrilla  Morgan  bad  entered  Frankfort, 
Ky.,  with  3000;  but  was  surprised  and  dispersed 
by  Dumont. 

Louibvillb,  Oct.  10.— The  battle  on  Wednes- 
day this  side  of  Perrysville,  commenced  early  in 
the  morning  by  an  attack  of  the  enemy  on  Mc- 
cook's division.  The  fight  was  confined  to  the 
artillery  till  about  2  o'clock,  p.  m.,  when  the  Fed- 
eral reinforcements  arrived,  and  a  charge  was 
made  on  the  rebels,  driving  them,  after  hard 
righting,  in  considerable  confusion.  The  1st 
Kentucky  Cavalry,  after  five  suooessive  charges, 
succeeded  in  capturing  two  rebel  batteries.  Our 
force,  before  the  arrival  of  reinforcements,  con- 
sisted of  about  15,000  infantry,  8  batteries  of  ar- 
tillery, and  2500  cavalry.  The  rebel  forces  have 
not  been  estimated,  There  is  no  reliable  account 
of  the  losses  on  either  side,  but  it  is  supposed  to 
be  heavy  from  the  large  mortality  among  the  field 
officers.  A  large  ambulance  train  left  to-day  to 
convey  the  wounded  to  Louisville.  Among  the 
Federals  killed,  are  Gens.  Jackson  and  Terrill, 
Acting  Brigadiers  Webster  and  Little,  aud  Lieut. 
Cols.  Jewett  and  McCarnpbelL  Col.  Pope  is 
wounded,  and  Gen.  Rosseau  reported  wounded. 

Early  yesterday  morning  Gen.  Buell  attacked 
Bragg  s  forces  at  Champliu  Creek,  in  the  imme- 
diate vicinity  ef  Perrysville.  A  short  but  terrific 
fight  ensued,  when  the  rebels  broke  and  retreated 
over  diverging  roads  southward.  Our  forces  are 
close  in  pursuit,  and  it  is  hoped  will  bag  the  whole 
lot.  At  last  accounts  part  of  Gen.  Gilbert's  forces 
were  in  their  rear  and  part  below  them.

one  of  the  cleanest  of  the  war.  The  enthusiasm  of 
Rosekrans'  array  is'  boundless.  We  have  2000 
prisoners,  including  100  officers.

The  proposition  to  conciliate  (be  Pope  and 
the  Italian  Government  have  proved  unac- 
ceptable at  Home.

ought  to  leave  Rome,  no  one  supposes  that

Lillooet  News. 

Lit. Loo kt,  Oct.  13,  1862. 

Judge  Begbie  and  Mr.  Greville  Mathew, 
also  Mr.  H.  P.  Walker,  Barrister,  arrived  here  on 
the  12th  inst  There  are  no  criminal  eases  on  the 
list;  a  few  civil  applications  in  chambers  only  arc 
to  bo  disposed  of. 

The  prisoner  Gilchrist,  found  guilty  of 'man- 
slaughter in  tbe  recent  shooting  affray  at  Williams 
Lake,  and  sentenced  to  penal  servitude  for  life,  is 
on  his  way  down  with  Lock,  formerly  of  the  firm 
of  Lock  &  Hart,  and  the  notorious  "Brooke/." 

I  can  also  state  from  a  strictly  reliable  souree.that 
the  rumor  of  the  Judge  having  bought  into  claims 
in  Cariboo  is  fallacious.  The  Judge  is  not  a  free 
miner ,-  has  no  certificate ;  and  caunot  hold  any 
claim. 

One  of  the  Canadian  companies,  whose  claim 
adjoins  the  Albion  or  Campbell  &  Co.,  on  Williams 
Creek,  has  been  bought  out  for  $9000.  Great  ex- 
citement prevails  on  this  creek,  on  the  fiat  below 
the  canon— every  foot  of  ground  is  taken  np  for 
two  miles  down.  The  exodus  for  the  season  baa 
fairly  set  in.  Our  hotels  are  crowded  with  luokv 
fellows.  On  Saturday  $500,000  was  brought  here 
and  deposited  iu  the  safe  of  a  storekeeper  j  by 
miners  who  all  came  down  together.  Yesterday 
I  knew  of  $200,000.  To-day  everybody  has  been 
to  see  a  race  for  $300  a  side,  between  one  of  the 
Lillooet  "cracks"  and  an  up  country  horse,  iu 
which  the  latter  came  off  victorious. 

Our  regular  fall  races  are  advertised  as  coming 
off  this  week. 

Notwithstanding  the  immense  quantities  of  gold 
coming  down— horses  packed  with  gold  and  noth- 
ing else,  reminding  me  of  the  mule-packs  of  silver 
In  Lima— there  arc  no  robberies  committed;  .a 
fact,  the  road  is  "all  serene,"  and  so  mote  it  be. 

F.  W.  F. 

Nanaimo  Letter. 

Nanaimu,  V.  I.,  6th  Oct.,  1862. 

Editor BarriSH  Colonist:— You  always  learn, 
through  some  avenue  or  other,  when  there  is  any- 
thing being  done  in  the  Police  Court  here,  and 
though  it  may  seem  unnecessary  to  let  you  know 
wbeu  nothing  is  being  done,  it  will  prove  how  ver- 
itable were  the  predictions  of  the  Colonist  when 
speaking  of  the  appointment  of  a  stipendiary  mag- 
istrate for  this  place,  namely :  "that  he  is  no  more 
wanted  than  a  fifth  wheel  in  a  coach,"  and  that  a 
non-stipendiary  or  non-salaried  magistrate  would 
suit  much  better,  in  what  respects  I  need  not  state, 
we  all  know.  yftri 

Only  one  ease  has  been  tried  during  the  past 
five  weeks,  whioh  occupied  the  magistrate  about 
(en  minutes.  I  hare  heard  that  '*  Died  of  having 
nothing  to  do,"  was  the  tombstone  record  of  a  re- 
spectable idler  in  bygone  days.  Is  notour  learned 
stipendiary  magistrate,  I  would  ask,  in  a  fair  way 
of  having  his  dissolution  caused  by  the  same  com- 
plaint? 

Nanaimo  pays  to  the  treasury  some  £600  annu- 
ally, yet  not  the  first  shilling  is  expended  here  by 
Government  on  roads  or  other  improvements.  A 
good  few  shillings  arc  expended  on  the  most  use- 
less official  over  thrust  among  a  community,  and 
scarcely  one  has  he  earned  the  last  five  weeks. 
What  dodges  have  been  attempted  to  insure  pick- 
ings for  this  over-worked  individual  ?  Port  of  En- 
try affair,  Small  Debts'  Court ;  and  then  he  is  to 
be  regarded  as  a  government  agent,  which  length- 
ens his  signature  to  J.  P.  and  «e.,  Ac,  Ac.  Then 
there  is  the  allowance  for  rent  of  court  room,  and 
for  stationery.  All  these  dodges  combined  show 
thetincongruity  of  his  position ;  and  however  the 
man  can  unblushingly  reside  among  and  frown 
upon  the  people  from  whom  bis  salary  is  exacted, 
and  receive  in  such  a  way  their  hard  earnings  for 
doing  next  to  nothing,  I  am  at  a  loss  to  imagine. 
Is  not  this  a  waste  of  public  funds  ?  Are  the  peo- 
ple to  be  taxed,  and  submit  to  such  superfluous 
functionaries  without  murmuring  ? 

Tbe  energetic  manager  here  thinks  it  a  mean 
proeeedlng  to  communicate  to  you  without  giving 
a  real  name,  whioh  correspondents  seldom  do,  for 
reasons  best  known  to  themselves.  However,  Mr. 
Editor,  one  case  in  five  weeks,  remember,  for 
whioh  credit  is  given.  But  as  it  is  denied  that  the 
magistrate  is  acting  in  any  capacity  belonging  to 
the  police  department,  can  you  tell  us  in  what  ca- 
pacity ho  does  act  ?  For  my  part,  I  assert,  and  -• 
is  plainly  shown,  that  he  acts  but  very  little  in  any 
capacity. 

You  may  expect,  Mr.  Editor,  to  get  both  tho 
bright  and  dark  side  of  any  question  concerning 
the  people  of  Nanaimo,  from  your

Tuesday.  October  9H,  MM. 

_  A  Good  Time  Coming. 

When  Gav.  Douglas  was  at  Yale  the  good 
people  of  that  promising  town  tendered  him 
a  very  hearty  reception.  Powder  was  burnt ; 
itnd  an  address  was  presented  that  far  ex- 
ceeded in  expressious  of  confidence  and  admi- 
ration the  famous  address  of  the  New  West- 
minster "Hyacks."  The  Governor  was  compli- 
mented for  pushing  the  Yale-Lytton  road 
through,  in  spite  ot  the  opinions  of  "  accom- 
plished engineers."  In  fact  the  Yale  people 
battered  His  Excellency  so  well  with  praise 
that  the  despot's  heart  relented.  Even  the 
mule-remonstrances  aud  addresses  of  last  fall 
and  winter  were  forgotten ;  and  the  Governor 
expressed  himself  as  deeply  affected — so  un- 
usual was  it,  no  doubt,  for  His  Excellency  to 
be  flattered  by  bis  "  faithful  subjects  "  in  that 
-  bleak  and  desolute  region."  The  Yalo  folks 
are  a  practical  people,  indeed  !  They  melted 
down  the  icy  barrier  between  the  governed 
and  the  Governor ;  and  the  old  problem  of 

%      "  yon  tickle  me  and  I'll  tickle  you "  was 
worked  out  like  a  oharm.    All  the  Yaleitw 
/asked  was  not  only  granted  ;  but  far  away  be- 
yond—the luture  was  pictured  in  the  most 
^    roseate  hue  imaginable.    His  Excellency  said 

t  "  he  had  recommended  to  the  Home  Govern- 
ment organic  changes  in  the  mode  of  ad  mini*, 
tration,  in  a  liberal  and  progressive  sense." 
The  "  organic  change  "  does  not  seem  to  mean 
a  change  of  Governor  ;  but  that  a  Council 
or  a  representative  Assembly  would  take  the 
place  of  the  present  desi  otism.  As  a  result 
of  those  contemplated  changes  the  Executive 
expects  to  be  in  some  respects  partially  re- 
lieved from  bis  responsibilities.  Would  it  bo 
strange  if  such  a  body  relieved  him  entirely  1 
Next  January,  or  thereabouts,  is  the  time 
fixed  for  the  organic  change  to  take  effect. 
Aspirants  for  political  distinction  in  British 
Columbia  may  well  prepare  themselves  tor 
political  honors,  as  but  a  tew  months  can 
elapse  before  they  may  wear  them.  Even 
before  the  delegate  reaches  Albion,  "  the 
good  time  a-coming  "  shall  have  arrived- 
Whilst  he  is  probably  thundering  at  the 
doors  of  the  Colonial  office,  the  enfran- 
chisement of  the  people  of  British  Columbia 
will  be  unfait  accompli.  Nothing  in  reality 
will  remain  for  the  delegate  really  to  trans- 
act bat  to  get  the  Governor  displaced  and  a 
new  resident  Governor  installed  at  the  vice- 
regal palace,  that  is  to  be,  at  New  West- 
minster. We  certainly  congratulate  the  Gov 
crnor  on  getting  ahead  of  his  dutiful  and 

*  loyal  subjects  iu  hasteoiug  the  inauguration  of 
"  liberal  and  progressive  principles."  It  is  an 
admission  that  such  a  reform  is  much  wanted  ; 
bat  at  the  same  time  it  is  hoped  that  it  may 
bave  the  power  as  well  as  the  form  of  a  re- 
form. In  other  words  it  is  hoped  that  the 
British  Columbian  Upper  and  Lower  House 

may  not  be  stoffed  with  the  tools  of  the  Ex-

Mr.  Cary  then  acknowledged  that  he  had  com 
mitted  an  error  in  the  proposed  tax.  His  object 
had  been  to  tax  the  man  not  the  goods.  Ha  in- 
tendep  not  to  devote  the  additional  revenue  to  the 
Government  but  to  the  rendering  the  resources 
of  the  colony  available.  The  colony  had  hitherto 
owed  more  to  Providence  than  any  other  country- 
he  had  beard  of.  Ha  wished  soon  to  have  mouay 
with  which  to  subsidise  a  steamer  to  visit  the  coast 
settlements.    (Hear,  hear.] 

Mr.  Foster  said  he  had  frequently  heard  Mr. 
Cary  declare  that  if  Vancouver  Island  ever  attained 
greatness  it  would  owe  its  elevation  to  the  Frre 
Port  ay  stem.  He  waa  therefore  astonished  at  the 
proposition  for  encroachment  just  enunciated. 
He  agreed,  hitherto  the  colony  had  almost  been 
left  to  providence,  but  did  not  approve  of  aueh 
negligence.  He  regretted  the  Government  did 
not  possess  the  confience  of  the  House.  He  then 
alluded  to  the  circumstance  of  government  taking 
advantage  of  the  mistake  of  the  merchants  by  as- 
cepting  the  return  including  consigned  merchan- 
dise which  he  considered  almost  a  swindle. 

Mr.  Cary  thought  thia  expression  uncalled  for. 
He  waa  not  aware  until  jual  now  of  the  fact.  Per- 
haps even  now  it  could  not  be  proved.

the  work  on  the  Esquimau  road  would  be  pro- 
ceeded with,  remarkiug  that  the  daily  increasing 
traffic  between  Esquimau  and  Victoria,  as  well  as 
the  lateness  of  the  season  .demsnded  that  the  under- 
taking be  commenced  without  delay. 

Mr.  Cary  replied  that  arrangements  had  been  com- 
pleted that  had  but  awsited  the  return  of  the  Gov- 
ernor from  British  Columbia  to  be  effected.  The 
worka  on  the  Esqaimalt  road  would  in  all  proba- 
bility be  commenced  in  the  beginning  of  next 
week  ;  nor  on  that  road  only.
