Mr.  Burnaby  rose  to  draw  attention  to  the  way 
in  which  the  Free  Port  system  would  be'endan- 
gered,  if  this  section  passed  the  House. 

Mr.  Cary  thought  that  the  principle  of  the  Free 
Fort  waa  in  no  danger.  He  did  not  think  that  the 
imposition  of  a  tax  where  goods  were  sold  would 
diminish  the  advantages  of  the  system.  In  the 
ease  of  duties,  the  importer  had  to  pay  cash  or 
aubmit  to  his  goods  being  bonded  before  they  could 
be  sold ;  by  the  tax  on  sales,  however,  the  " 
porter  was  only  called  upon  to  pay  the  duty  after 
converting  bis  merchandise  into  cash.  By  pur- 
suing this  system  the  chief  vices  of  the  custom 
house  system  would  be  avoided.  Hitherto  the 
revenue  had  been  collected  in  a  cheese-paring 
sort  of  way.  Should  we  continue  on  with  the  present 
pauper-like  condition  of  the  Treasury  ?  If  it  did 
interfere  with  the  Free  Port  system,  the  imposition 
was  small— it  would  not  be  felt — and  yet  be  adding 
a  steady  and  inoreasing  revenue  to  the  Colony  that 
would  cost  no  trouble  to  collect.  Goods  imported 
by  the  merchants  had  hitherto  paid  towards  the 
revenue,  but  consigned  goods  had  escaped.  Meas- 
ures must  be  taken  to  increase  the  revenue  of  the 
Colony. 

Mr.  Burnaby  begged  to  state  that  every  mer- 
chant had  hitherto  returned  and  paid  the  tax  upon 
his  business  transactions,  including  consigned 
goods  among  those  specially  imported. 

Mr.  Cary  said  if  this  were  the  case  they  had 
paid  more  than  they  were  asked ;  and  asserted 
that  from  the  wording  of  the  former  act  consigned 
goods  had  not  been  included,  and  appealed  to 
Mr.  King  toconfirm  his  opinion,  which  the  learned 

'  lfi.  Burnaby  and  the  other  members  however

OUR  LONDON  LETTER. 

[FBOM  Ot'K  LOXDOX  CORRESPONDENT.] 

London,  Sept.  13, 1862. 
I  need  scarcely  tell  yoo  that  the  one  absorb- 
ing theme  of  the  last  fortnight  bus  been  the 
rapture,  wounding  and  probable  fate  of  the 
noblest  of  modern  men  of  action — General 
Garibaldi.  We  have  cow,  perhaps,  as  full 
details  of  this  unfortunate  affair  as  wo  shall 
ever  have,  unless,  indeed,  Garibaldi  himself  is 
spared]  to  write  the  complete  history  ol  it, 
or  is  induced  to  give  the  particulars  to  some 
one  who  can  and  will  write  it  for  the  satisfac- 
tion of  the  friends  of  the  Italian  canse,  and 
the  confusion  ol  an  ill-osed  and  much  calum- 
niated people.  Garibaldi  h  is.  in  a  letter  to 
some  of  his  friends,  given  a  ra  id  sketch  of  the 
scene  when  he  was  captured,  and  the  motives 
which  actuated  bim.  This  it-tter  deserves  a 
place  here,  and  will  be  read  with  intense  in- 
terest by  every  right-minded  man  and  loving- 
hearted  woman.  It  speaks  volumes  for  the 
disinterested  Italian  patriot,  and  has  already 
raised  him  more  than  a  hundred  per  cent,  in 
the  estimation  of  all  in  this  country. 

CARIBALDia   NARRATIVE   OF  THE  ASMtOMOKTE 
CONFLICT. 

The  following  letter  is  from  Garibaldi  him- 
self, relating  to  the  conflict  at  Aspromonte  : 

ON  BOARD  THE  DUKE  OF  GENOA,  > 

 September  L  >  

Thev  thirsted  for  blood,  nod  I  wished  to 
spare  it.  Not  the  poor  soldiers  who  obeyed 
but  the  men  of  the  clique  who  cannot  forgive 
the  revolution  for  being  the  revolution — it  is 
that  which  disturbs  their  conservative  diges- 
tion— and  for  having  contributed  to  the  re- 
establishment  of  onr  Italian  family. 

Yes,  they  thirsted  for  bloed  ;  I  perceived  it 

to  the  utmost  to  prevent  that  of  our  assailants 
from  being  shed. 

I  ran  to  the  front  of  our  line  crying  out  to 
them  not  to  fire,  and  from  the  centre  to  the 
left,  where  my  voice  and  those  of  my  aides  de- 
camp could  be  heard,  not  a  trigger  was  pulled. 
It  was  not  thus  on  the  attacking  side.  Having 
arrived  at  a  distance  of  200  metres,  they  be- 
gan a  tremendous  fire,  and  the  party  of  bersag- 
lieri  who  were  in  front  ol  me  directing  their 


shots  against  me,  struck  me  with  two  balls, 
one  in  the  left  thigh,  not  serious,  the  other  in 
the  ankle  of  the  right  foot,  making  a  serious 
wound. 

Aa  all  this  happened  at  the  opening  of  the 
conflict,  and  I  was  carried  to  the  skirt  of  the 
wood  after  being  wounded,  I  could  see  nothing 
more,  a  thick  crowd  having  formed  around  me 
while  my  wound  was  being  dressed.  I  feel 
certain,  however,  that  up  to  the  end  of  the 
lice  which  was  at  my  litter,  and  to  that  of  my 
aides-de-camp,  not  a  single  musket  was  fired. 
As  there  was  no  firing  en  our  side,  it  was  easy 
for  the  troops  to  approach  and  mingle  with 
onrs,  and  wln>n  I  was  told  that  they  wished  to 
disarm  us,  I  replied  that  the  men  might  dis- 
arm themselves.  The  intentions  of  my  com- 
panions were,  however,  so  little  hostile  that  I 
only  by  this  disarmed  in  the  crowd  some  officers 

It  was  not  no  ou  our  right.  The  picciotti, 
attacked  by  the  regular  troops,  replied  by  a 
fire  upon  the  whole  line,  and  although  the 
trumpets  sounded  to  eeane  firing,  there  was 
at  that  place  a  smart  fusillade,  which  lasted 
not  more  than  a  quarter  of  an  hour. 

My  wounds  led  to  sorao  confusion  in  our 
line.  Our  soldiers,  not  seeing  me,  began  to 
retreat  into  the  wood,  so  that  little  by  little 
the  crowd  round  me  broke  up,  and  the  most 
faithful  alone  remained.  At  this  moment  1 
learned  that  my  staff  and  Colonel  Pallavicini, 
who  commanded  the  regular  troops,  wore 
negotiating  upon  the  following  considera- 
tions: First  that  1  should  be  free  with  my 
•taff  to  withdraw  where  I  pleased.  ( I  replied, 
••On  board  an  English  vessel")  Second, 
that  having  arrived  at  the' sea  shore,  the  rest 
of  my  companions  should  be  set  at  liberty. 
Colonel  Pallavicini  conducted  himself  ns  a 
valorous  and  intelligent  chief  iq  all  his  mili- 
tary movements,  and  he  has  not  been  want- 
ing in  respect  or  courtesy  to  me  or  my  peo- 
pie.  He  showed  his  grief  in  having  to  shed 
Italian  blood  ;  but  he  had  received  peremp- 
tory orders  and  had  to  obey  them.  My  ar- 
rangements had  been  purely  defensive,  and 
1  had  hoped  to  avoid  a  conflict,  aseing  the 
vary  strong  position  that  I  occupied  and  en- 
tertaining; the  hope  tbat^the  regular  troops 
bad  receired  orders  lea*  sanguinary.  If  I 
had  uot  been  wounded  at  the  outset,  and  if 
my  people  had  not  received  the  orders  under 
all  etrcumstaecra  to  avoid  any  collision  what- 
soever with  the  regular  troops,  the  contest 

btHoVribto* Hoover! uV.' 
Whatever  may  be  the  result  of  my  wouads, 
whatever  fate  the  Government  prepares  for 
me,  I  have  the  consoiousnesa  of  having  done 
my  duty;  and  the  sacrifice  of  my  life  i*  a 
very  little  thing  if  it  has  contributed  to  rave 
that  of  a  great  number  of  my  fellow-oountry- 
auen. 

In  the  hazardous  enterprise  in  wbieh  I 
and  my  companions  bad  thrown  ourselves, 
with  heada  Dent,  t  hoped  nothing  good  from 
the  Government  of  Rutazsi.  But  why  should 
I  not  have  hoped  for  leas  rigor  on  the  part 
ef  the  King,  having  altered  in  nothing  tho 
old  programme,  and  having  decided  not  to 
alter  it  at  any  price  ?  What  afflicts  me  most 
is  lh  a  fatal  distrust,  which  contributes  not  a 
little  to  the  incorapletion  of  national  unity. 
However  it  may  be,  I  once  again  present  to 
Italy  a  serene  front,  assured  of  having  done 
my  duty.  Once  more,  my  unimportant  life 
and  the  more  precious  ones  of  so  manv  gen- 
erous young  men  have  been  offered  as  a 
holocaust  to  the  holiest  of  causes — pure  from 
all  vile  personal  interests. 

G.  Garibaldi. 

A  detailed  report  of  the  affair  at  Atpro- 
znente  has  been  published,  signed  l>y  four- 
teen officers  composing  the  staff  of  Garibal- 
di. In  thi<  st  .lenient  the  General's  force  is 
stated  to  have  been  1500  men.  The  ration* 
wbieh  hs/1  been  distributed  were  very  scanty, 
and  the  volunteer*  had  suffered  much  the 
previous  night  from  heavy  rains.  Thoy  bad 
express  and  formal  orders  never  to  attack  or 
defend  themselves,  but  to  march  rapidly — 
nothing  morow  On  (he  approach  of  the  troops, 
Garibaldi  sent  officers  along  the  line  to  im- 
previa  this  «f,.»  upon  his  men. 


Garibaldi  was  in  the  centre  of  the  hill  slope 
occupied  by  his  column.   He  seut  his  officers 
all  along  too  front  with  repeated,  express, 
positive  orders  not  to  fire.    Wo  were  being 
surrounded  on  all  sides ;  the  bersaglieri  were 
within  cbot ;  they  had  levelled  their  pieces; 
all  our  column  wus  perfectly  still.   Not  one 
about,  not  one  shot.   The  general  alone  stood 
up.  with  his  wide  gray  plaid  cloak  lined  with 
red,  thrown  on  his  massive  shoulders,  follow- 
ing the  movements  of  the  Royalists  with  his 
spy  glass,  and  from  time  to  time  turning  to 
repeat  the  order  "  Do  not  fire."   The  officers 
took  up  the  cry  aud  "  Do  not  fire"  went  the 
round  of  the  wholo  lino.    But  tho  troops  had, 
on  the  contrary,  precise  orders  to  attack. 
The  bersaglieri  opened  fire  and  moved  for- 
ward.  Ne  intimation  or  summons  proceeded 
the  fire,  no parlementaire  was  sent.   The  tir- 
ing deepened  ;  the  bullets  hissed  on  all  sides 
round  our  heads.   Unfortunately  some  of  our 
raw  recruits,  unaccustomed  to  such  terrible 
sport,  answered  by  a  few  random  shots  ;  the 
others  did  not  stir.   Every  one  kept  his  own 
ground,  some  standing,  some  seated.   All  the 
trumpets  gave  the  signal  to  step  fire  ;  all  the 
officers  verbally  issued  the  same  order.  The 
troops,  oo  the  contrary,  set  up  the  signal 
"  Forward,"  and  advanced  with  a  well-sus- 
tained fire.   The  general,  always  at  his  post, 
standing  in  tho  midst  of  the  densest  shower  of 
balls,  again  cried,  "  Do  not  fire!"   He  was 
uttering  those  words  when  two  bullets  struck 
him— one,  a  spent  ball,  on  the  thigh  ef  the 
left  leg  ;  another,  with  full  force,  on  the  ancle 
of  the  left  foot.   Garibaldi,  at  the  moment  of 
being  wonoded.  not  only  stood  up,  upright, 
but  be  assumed  a  majestic  attitude  ;  he  took 
off  bis  hat,  and  waving  it  with  his  left  hand, 
he  repeatedly  cried,  44  Loop  live  Italy  !  Do 
not  tiie  I"  Some  ot  tbe  officers,  the  nearest 
to  hiui,_  removed  him  and  laid  him  under  a 
Tree.   Thefe7  wllfi'fiw " baoTtual  calmness,  he 
continued  to  give  his  orders.   Tbe  most  pre- 
cise were  tbe  following  :— — Let  them  come 
near.    Do  not  fire!"   On  all  our  frout  the 
fire  hud  ceased.    Presently   Mcnotti  was 
brought  to  tbe  spot.   He  also  had  been  bit 
by  a  spent  bull  in  the  calf  of  his  left  lejr.  He 
was  in  great  pain,  unable  to  stand.  Father 
and  son  were  laid  under  the  same  tree  ;  u 
group  of  officer*  and  soldiers  gathered  round 
the  general.   He  bad  lighted  a  cigar,  and  was 
sinokitig.   He  said  to  all,  "  Do  not  fight !" 
The  officers,  questioned  by  their  soldiers,  also 
invariably  answered,  u  Do  not  fight."  The 
trumpets,  too,  never  ceased  from  their  signal, 
"  Stop  fire !"  not  lor  our  men.  but  for  tbe 
troops  wbicb  fired  as  they  advanced,  even 
when  they  had. come  up  aud  were  mixed  with 
our  volunteers.    From  the  first  shot  to  this 
moment  hardly  a  quarter  of  an  hour  elapsed. 
Here  a  strange  sight  presented  itself.  Friends, 
relatives,  brothers,  companions  iu  recent  bat- 
tles which  tbey  fought  lor  their  fatherland, 
meet  and  recognise  each  other.   Some  are 
clad  in  the  red  shirt,  others  in  the  regular 
uniform  ;  anu  here  were  shaking  of  hands, 
embraces,  mutual  reproach  and  upbraidiogs, 
especially  on  the  part  of  the  redshirts.  who 
protest  and  declare  that  "all  tbey  wanted 
was  Rome." 

The  narative  proceeds,  stating  that  here  a 
lieutenant  of  tbe  staff  (Royal)  appeared  be 
fore  Garibaldi,  who  bade  him  put  off  his 
sword,  as  a  parlementaire  should  be  unarm- 
ed. Other  bersaglieri  officers  were  disarmed 
iu  the  same  manner,  though  the  general 
•oon  afterwards  ordered  that  their  swords 
a'.iould  be  restored  to  them.  Meanwhile  the 
■urgeons  examined  and  dressed  the  gen- 
eral's wounds.  He  bade  tbem  apply  cold 
water  to  them,  and  all  the  time  he  smoked 
with  great  calmness  and  firmness.  He  ask- 
ed whether  an  amputation  was  necessary, 
in  which  case  it  should  be  done  forthwith. 
The  doctors  assured  him  there  was  no  oc- 
casion for  such  an  operation.  Garibaldi  ask- 
ed to  see  Pallavicini,  who  twenty  minutes 
later  came  to  bim  uncovered,  and  with 
every  demonstration  of  respect.  It  was 
afterwards  settled  between  the  officers  of 
both  staffs  that  the  Garibaldian  column 
should  be  disarmed  and  placed  under  the 
escort  of  the  Royal  troops.  It  was  agreed 
that  Garibaldi  should  be  removed  to  Scylla, 
with  as  many  of  hia  officers  as  he  wished  to 
have  with  bim.  all  of  whom  should  retain 
tbeir  swords.  The  dead  on  both  sides  were 
very  few  ;  very  few  also  the  wounded. 

Your  renders  can  easily  imagine  tbe  pro- 
found sympathy  their  brief  bat  eventful  his- 
tory has  produced  in  the  minds  of  ail  true 
burn  Britons.  The  reported  state  of  Gari- 
baldi's health  has  caused  great  alarm.  His 
friends  in  England  fear  that,  he  is  in  a  more 
precarious  condition  than  appears  from  the 
eurt  and  unsatisfactory  telegrams  which 
reach  ns  from  day  to  day.  A  few  of  his 
more  loving  friends  have  already  gone  in  the 
hopes  of  seeing  him.  Mr.  James  Stansfield, 
M.  P.,  whose  olear  and  rigorous  intellect, 
and  deep  sympathetic  nature  have  so  often 
been  employed  in  expounding  and  advocat- 
ing the  claims  of  tne  Italian  people,  left 
Loudon  at  the  beginning  of  this  week,  in 
company  with  Mr.  W.  H.  Ashurst,  Gari- 
baldi's treasurer  in  England,  Mr.  P.  A. 
Taylor,  M.  P.?  and  two  ether  friend*,  for 
Italy,  in  ordar,  if  possible,  to  gain  access  to 
tbe  wounded  bero  and  whisper  into  bis  ear 
expressions  of  deep  sympathy  for  him  in  ad- 
versity. This  step  is  one  dictated  by  feel- 
ings creditable  to  humanity.  Here  friends 
will  be  the  means,  let  ns  hope,  of  raising 
tbe  spirits  of  ihe  fallen  Hercules,  and  of  ac- 
complishing for  him  who  knows  more  than 
even  tbe  doctors. 

Since  this  deputation  left,  however,  the 
telegrams  received  here  excited  Ibe  alarms 
of  other  friends,  and  yielding  to  tbe  impulse 
of  delicate  and  anxious  feelings,  a  skillf  ul 
English  surgeon,  Professor  Partridge,  was 
on  Thursday  night  last  despatched  to  Spez- 
sia.  Lord  Palmerston  was  appealed  to  by 
two  London  gentlemen  and  induced  to  tele- 
grapu  to  6ii  James  Hudson,  in  order  that 
every  obstacle  might  bo  remove'd  out  of  the 
way  of  Professor  Parti idge  seeing  Garibaldi. 
Your  readers  may  perchance  have  a  higher 
idea  ol  this  act  of  love  on  the  part  of  a  few 
enthusiastic  young  men  when  I  tell  them 
that  tho  fee  of  Dr.  Partridge  fur  this  visit 
alone  is  £500,  and  £100  for  an  ex- 
perienced dresser  to  accompany  him.  The 
visit  will  cost  at  least  £600,  but  were  it 
twice  the  amount  the  money  wuuid  be  forth- 
coming in  a  few  day*. 

Meetings  of  sympathy  have  been  held  at 
Newcastle  and  elsewhere,  and  now  that  the 
enthusiasm  is  awakened,  ample  funds  will 
be  provide  i  ay  tie  English  people  to  pro 
vide  every  meant  of  comfort,  not  only  for 
Garibaldi  himself,  but  for  all  his  wounded 
companions  also.  When  ihe  heart  of  man 
is  once  touched  as  with  a  live  coal  from  the 
altar  of  humanity,  no  gift  is  to  >  precious  for 


those  loved.  Garibaldi  has  done  more  than 
any  modern  man,  save  Mazzicl,  to  bring  us 
back  to  the  ways  of  God  and  of  manhood, 
and  perhaps  hia  present  defeat  will  do  the 
present  age  more  good  than  all  his  past  vic- 
tories. It  is  surely  at  such  times  as  the 
present  that  we  feel  it  is  more  blessed  to 
give  than  to  receive. 

AMERICAN  WAR. 

After  reaching  such  a  human  Pisgah,  I  am 
loth  to  come  down  to  tho  vulgarity  of  tbe 
American  war,  and  the  sordid  notioos.it  unfor- 1 
tunately  suggests.  The  customs  of  journalism,  j 
however,  are  inexorable,  and  the  commands 
given  to  a  correspondent  are  imperative.  A 
newspaper  writer  is  not  tbe  only  mortal  who  is 
compelled  to  do  what  is  not  agreeable.  Too 
often— oftener  perhaps  than  need  be— he  has 
to  descend  Irom  tho  sublime  to  the  ridiculous  ; 
and  does  not  this  stupid  American  war,  from 
*  first  to  last,  look  superlatively  ridiculous  ?  It 
j  was  hoped  that  tbe  North  would  do  something 
to  redeem  the  Yankee  character ;  but  tbe 
hope  bus  been  so  long  deferred  that  the  heart 
of  England  at  least  is  sick.  Well,  there  has 
been  another  battle  at  Bull  Run  ;  but  1  expect 
that  you  will  have  all  the  news  about  it  long 
before  this  cau  reach  you.  Unexpectedly, 
therefore,  I  am  saved  the  irksome  task  of 
writing  about  it  now. 

Tbe  distress  in  Lancashire  is  still  on  the  in- 
crease, and  the  benevolently  disposed  ure  taxed 
to  their  utmost  to  meet  the  demands  made 
upon  tbem.  Cotton  companies  however,  are 
now  almost  as  plentiful  as  blackberries,  and 
surely  before  very  much  longer  we  shall  have 
ample  supplies  ot  tbe  raw  material. 

The  Archbishop  of  Canterbury  died  on 
Saturday  morning  last,  after  a  lingering  ill- 
ness. He  attained  tbe  lipe  age  of  83,  and 
now  that  be  is  gone,  not  a  few  eager  clercal 
eyes  are  turned  towards  his  vacant  post.  It  is 
rumored  that  tbe  Bishop  of  London  will  be  bis 
successor.  Dr.  Tail  is  a  good  man.  and  would 
fill  the  post  with  credit  to  himself  and  to  the 
satisfaction  of  good  men. 

The  descent  from  the  notice  of  an  arch- 
bishop to  that  of  abeep  is  great,  but  it  need 
not  necessarily  suggest  impure  thoughts  to 
any  coloniul  reader.  Here,  in  Englaud.  the 
death  of  sheep  is  excitiug  very  serious  concern 
indeed.  The  loathsome  and  fatal  disease  of 
small-pox  is  in  some,  counties  cutting  them 
down  with  alarming  rapidity.  If  we  mourn 
over  tbe  death  of  man,  we  cannot  withhold  a 
regret  for  the  decay  of  those  animals  whi»h 
form  a  staple  of  man's  life  A  Government 
iuspector  has  been  appointed  to  inquire  into 
the  nature  at  the  disease  among  sheep,  with 
the  laudable  view  of  preventing,  as  far  as  pos- 
sible, its  further  spread.  The  cause  of  tbe 
plague  no  doubt  is  to  be  found  in  the  wet  nod 
blighting  summer  we  have  bad.  There  is 
nothing  mote  destructive  to  sheep  than  a  rainy 
season.  Though  the  supply  of  mutton  is  still 
plentiful,  there  are  fewer  chops  consumed,  for 
not  a  few  shrink  from  dining  on  mutton,  from 
a  natural  fear  of  catching  the  infection. 

The  International  Exhibition  loses  none  of 
its  popularity  as  the  time  draws  near  when  it 
is  to  close,  and  amoog  certain  classes  it  is  be- 
coming rather  a  favorite  lounge  for  spare 
hours  ;  and  people  are  at  last  finding  out  some 
method  in  its  many  courts  and  thoroughfares, 
and,  by  making  judicious  use  of  prominent 
landmarks,  are  able  to  discover  for  a  second 
time  every  object  of  interest.  The  contract- 
ors, Messrs.  Kelt  and  Lucas,  are  beginning  to 
be  more  hopeful  of  not  losing  anything  by 
their  rather  hazardous  speculation,  and  it  is 
now  almost  certain  that  there  will  be  no  pecu- 
niary loss  to  the  guarantors.  The  only  subject 
on  wbicb  there  is  still  much  doubt,  is  whether 
tbe  entire  buildiug  is  to  come  down,  and  all 
lovers  of  art  are  rather  anxious  lest  tbey 
should  lose  the  picture  gallery,  which  is  better 
suited  to  its  purpose  than  any  other  in  Eng- 
land, and  which  makes  ns  feel  more  alarmed 
than  ever  of  the  wretched  affair  in  Trafalgar 
Square,  called  the  National  Gallery  ! 

That  tbe  Exhibition  of  1862  is  a  decided 
improvement  on  its  predecessor  of  1851,  we  alt 
agree,  and  as  a  means  of  showing  us  how  we 
have  improved  during  that  time  by  learning 
from  our  neighbors,  no  better  scheme  could 
have  been  devised  ;  and  it  repeats  te  us  tbe  old 
lesson  that  tbe  more  we  learn  the  more  we  see 
how  many  are  the  things  of  which  we  are  in 
comparative  ignorance.  We  must  now  reckon 
on  them  periodically,  and  consider  them  as 
settled  institutions  amongst  us.
